- *established* — Communications without malice to an interested person by one also interested are conditionally privileged (California). [EV-E-022, Cal. Civ. Code §47(c)]
- *established* — Sponsors treat investigations of account holders as grounds to freeze accounts, showing investigation information is sponsor-held and sensitive. [EV-E-002, Termination of account privileges; EV-E-003]

**Supporting interpretation.** Propagating verified public-source facts (IRS revocation, Pub 78 removal, OFAC listing, court judgments) is low risk: truth and public-record status defeat defamation, and common-interest privilege covers communications among sponsors with pending grants to the charity. Sponsor-private investigation content is unverified opinion and confidential sponsor data; propagating it risks defamation (privilege lost if malice or excessive publication) and breaches multi-tenant confidentiality.

**Competing interpretation.** Rev. Proc. 2018-32 §4.04 suggests that when one sponsor learns of facts showing imminent revocation, other sponsors' reliance could be affected if they are told; withholding risk signals could leave other sponsors exposed. Also, coordinated declines among sponsors could raise group-boycott (antitrust) questions (interpretation; not researched).

**Applicability to Unbound.** The platform is the conduit; it should propagate system holds tied to public facts and a neutral 'review requested' signal, never another tenant's private evidence.

**Business assumptions.**

- Sponsors are separate tenants with contractual confidentiality.
- Public facts are re-verified independently from the source before propagation (SPD-GRT-002).

**Recommended answer.** Confirm: propagate (a) holds that derive from verified public facts (IRS revocation/auto-revocation, Pub 78 removal, OFAC SDN match, published court judgments) with source and retrieval timestamp, re-verified by the platform from the primary source; (b) never propagate sponsor-private investigation content, suspicions or decisions; (c) optionally allow a sponsor to share an anonymised, neutral 'enhanced review recommended' flag only under a written inter-sponsor information-sharing agreement that recites the common interest, limits recipients to sponsors with pending or recent grants to the charity, and contains no factual allegations.

**Remaining uncertainty.** Qualified privilege rules differ by state; antitrust exposure of coordinated declines not researched; no case law on DAF sponsor information sharing found.

**Consequences if wrong.** Propagating private allegations could bring defamation and confidentiality claims; failing to propagate public facts could lead to taxable distributions or grants to revoked charities.

**Conservative fallback.** Propagate only public-source facts; no inter-sponsor flags at all.

**Alternative business or architecture design.** Each sponsor sees only primary-source public data refreshed by the platform (no sponsor-originated signals): minimal legal exposure, loses early-warning value.

**External authorization required.** Written inter-sponsor information-sharing agreement only if the optional neutral flag is enabled.

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given sponsor A records an internal investigation note on a charity, Then no field of that note is visible to sponsor B via UI, API, export or logs.
- Given the IRS auto-revocation list adds a charity, Then a hold is placed for every sponsor with open grants to it, citing the IRS source and retrieval time.
- Given a public fact is reported by a sponsor, Then it propagates only after the platform re-verifies it from the primary source.

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- Defamation claim or regulator inquiry
- Inter-sponsor information-sharing agreement adopted
- Antitrust guidance on charitable sector coordination

**Related questions.**

- none

**Corrections to prior records.**

- none

### XID-CLM-002 — Confirm provisional decision: Legal basis, maximum duration and liability for protective suspension of a representative.

**Disposition:** business-policy-decision · **Confidence:** medium · **Jurisdiction:** Sponsor program terms (contract); federal §4966 control; FINRA 2165 and Senior Safe Act as analogies only; state APS reporting laws vary · **Batch:** E (Governance, succession and delegated authority)

**Original provisional answer.** The sponsor may place a protective suspension on a representative's advisory privileges when it reasonably believes exploitation or abuse of authority is occurring. Maximum 15 business days, one extension of up to 10 business days by an independent reviewer, then referral to counsel or the appropriate authority. No money moves during suspension; the principal is notified unless notice would put them at risk.

**Relevant requirements.** UNB-XIN-CLM-006, ADM-045, /home/user/unbound/requirements/administration/DAF-ADMINISTRATION-AND-SPONSOR-OPERATIONS.md, /home/user/unbound/requirements/discovery/2026-11-23-cross-industry/data/decisions.csv

**Authoritative findings.**

- *established* — Sponsors reserve the right to limit, suspend or revoke privileges in their sole discretion and to freeze accounts while an advisor is under investigation or subject to court orders. [EV-E-002, Termination of account privileges; EV-E-003 Account privilege limitations; EV-E-001 'Termination of Giving Account privileges']
- *established* — FINRA Rule 2165 permits holds on disbursements and securities transactions where exploitation of a Specified Adult is reasonably believed: 15 business days, +10 after internal review, +30 more if reported to a state regulator/agency or court; notice within 2 business days except to suspected parties. [EV-E-013, Rule 2165(b)(1)-(4)]
- *established* — The 30-business-day extension took effect 2022-03-17; a further amendment filing SR-FINRA-2026-018 is pending. [EV-E-014, Reg. Notice 22-05 (search-verified only)]
- *established* — Senior Safe Act gives immunity for good-faith reports to APS/regulators/law enforcement only to listed covered financial institutions and trained employees; it authorizes no holds; a DAF sponsor is not a listed institution. [EV-E-015, 12 USC 3423(a)(1)(D),(a)(2)]
- *established* — UPOAA §120(b)(6) lets a person refuse an acknowledged POA if it reports, or knows another has reported, suspected exploitation by the agent to APS. [EV-E-009, UPOAA §120(b)(6); NC §32C-1-120(c)(9)]
- *interpretation* — Because the sponsor has exclusive legal control and privileges are contractual, a time-bounded protective suspension needs no statutory safe harbor; its legal basis is the program terms. [EV-E-005, §4966(d)(2)(A)(ii)]

**Supporting interpretation.** The legal basis is contractual: published terms of Fidelity, Vanguard and DAFgiving360 already allow suspension in sole discretion with no time limit. A short, reviewed time limit is a self-imposed fairness control, not a legal requirement; FINRA 2165 is a sound template because it pairs a reasonable-belief trigger with notice, internal review and escalation.

**Competing interpretation.** A fixed 15+10 business-day cap could force release before an APS or court process completes; FINRA itself allows a further 30 business days once reported to a regulator or court. Conversely, an indefinite freeze without review may be criticized as unfair to a legitimate representative, though sponsor terms permit it.

**Applicability to Unbound.** Platform implements the hold, clocks, reviewer roles, notice templates and escalation; the sponsor decides. The platform must never auto-release a hold that has been escalated to APS, law enforcement or a court.

**Business assumptions.**

- Sponsors adopt program-terms language permitting protective suspension of representatives.
- No money moves during suspension except sponsor-approved protective actions.
- Sponsors may voluntarily report to APS; immunity depends on state APS law, not the Senior Safe Act.

**Recommended answer.** Confirm with amendments: legal basis = sponsor program terms (exclusive legal control), not statute. Trigger: reasonable belief of exploitation or abuse of authority. Initial suspension up to 15 business days; one extension up to 10 business days after documented independent review; a further extension up to 30 business days (or until resolution) only when the matter has been reported to APS, law enforcement, a regulator or a court, or a court/agency directs. Notice to the principal and other authorized parties within 2 business days unless they are suspected or notice would put the principal at risk. On expiry without escalation: release or convert to a sponsor revocation decision - never silent lapse. Liability: rely on program-terms sole-discretion and exculpation clauses; no federal immunity applies to sponsors.

**Remaining uncertainty.** State APS reporting/immunity statutes for charities not surveyed; no case law on DAF representative suspensions found; pending SR-FINRA-2026-018 may change the analogy.

**Consequences if wrong.** Too-short limits could release funds to an exploiter; too-long or unreviewed holds could prompt complaints or claims by legitimate representatives.

**Conservative fallback.** Program terms allow indefinite suspension at sponsor discretion with mandatory 15-business-day reviews and recorded reasons.

**Alternative business or architecture design.** Rely only on donor-pre-designated trusted contact and refuse representatives (see XID-TRS-001 option A): avoids most exploitation exposure; friction for families; loses ability to serve incapacitated donors through agents.

**External authorization required.** Sponsor approval of program-terms wording for protective suspension.

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given a protective suspension is placed, Then a 15-business-day expiry is set and the principal is notified within 2 business days unless the 'notice_risk' flag is set with a reason.
- Given an independent reviewer extends, Then the total cannot exceed 25 business days unless an escalation record (APS, law enforcement, regulator or court) exists.
- Given an escalation record exists, Then a further extension up to 30 business days or until a court/agency directs is permitted and the hold cannot auto-release.
- Given a suspension is active, Then no grant, transfer or privilege change by the representative is executed.
- Given expiry is reached without decision, Then the case escalates to the sponsor owner rather than releasing silently.

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- SEC approval of SR-FINRA-2026-018
- State legislation extending exploitation-hold safe harbors to charities
- Sponsor counsel guidance

**Related questions.**

- **UNB-XIN-CLM-006#Q1**: Legal basis is the sponsor's program terms under its exclusive legal control; max 15 business days + 10 on independent review, + up to 30 more only after report to APS/law enforcement/regulator/court; documented authority, reason, owner, review deadline and escalation; never auto-release a legally required or escalated hold.

**Corrections to prior records.**

- XID-CLM-002 decision_outcome.authority ('FINRA Rule 2165 temporary hold: 15 business days, extendable 10'): Incomplete: since 2022-03-17 Rule 2165 allows a further 30 business days when reported to a state regulator/agency or court, and covers securities transactions as well as disbursements; a further amendment (SR-FINRA-2026-018) is pending. → Model 15 + 10 + 30 (escalated) business days, as an analogy only.

### XID-CLM-003 — Confirm provisional decision: Approve the public decline reason catalog and neutral codes.

**Disposition:** business-policy-decision · **Confidence:** high · **Jurisdiction:** US; sponsor program terms; state defamation law (qualified privilege); no federal adverse-action rule applies to DAF grant declines · **Batch:** E (Governance, succession and delegated authority)

**Original provisional answer.** Adopt a public decline reason catalog of neutral codes: ELIGIBILITY_NOT_CONFIRMED, ORGANIZATION_STATUS_CHANGED, PURPOSE_NOT_PERMITTED_BY_POLICY, IMPERMISSIBLE_BENEFIT, INFORMATION_NOT_RECEIVED, PAYMENT_DETAILS_UNVERIFIED, COMPLIANCE_REVIEW (restricted reasons, e.g. sanctions or investigations, always map here), FUND_BALANCE_INSUFFICIENT, DUPLICATE_REQUEST, SPONSOR_DISCRETION. Free-text explanations are optional and reviewed.

**Relevant requirements.** UNB-XIN-CLM-002, /home/user/unbound/requirements/administration/DAF-ADMINISTRATION-AND-SPONSOR-OPERATIONS.md, /home/user/unbound/requirements/discovery/2026-11-24-simplification-economics/11-CUSTOMER-FRICTION-ANALYSIS.md

**Authoritative findings.**

- *established* — Sponsors retain sole discretion over grants and may decline recommendations; DAFgiving360 notifies account holders when it declines a recommendation because a charity did not supply information within 30 days. [EV-E-003, Recommending grants (charity verification paragraph)]
- *established* — Fidelity may remove an organization from eligibility and limit privileges in its sole discretion; no published obligation to state reasons. [EV-E-001, Remedial actions; Timing of grant review]
- *interpretation* — No statute or IRS guidance found that requires a DAF sponsor to give a reason for declining a grant recommendation; ECOA/Reg B and NAIC Model 900 adverse-action rules do not apply to DAF grants (interpretation; cited as analogies only). [EV-E-001, (absence)]
- *established* — Statements about third parties (charities) shared with an interested person without malice are conditionally privileged in California; the privilege is lost on malice. [EV-E-022, Cal. Civ. Code §47(c)]

**Supporting interpretation.** Neutral codes that describe the sponsor's decision rather than assert facts about a charity reduce defamation exposure (truthful, non-accusatory) and avoid disclosing sanctions screening or investigations. Codes like ORGANIZATION_STATUS_CHANGED reflect public IRS data and are verifiable.

**Competing interpretation.** IMPERMISSIBLE_BENEFIT shown to a donor implies wrongdoing by the donor (not the charity) - acceptable because it concerns the donor's own recommendation, but wording should describe the rule ('Grant would provide a benefit not permitted by policy'). SPONSOR_DISCRETION and COMPLIANCE_REVIEW are opaque; donors may perceive unfairness, but no law requires more.

**Applicability to Unbound.** Platform ships the catalog as sponsor-configurable defaults with fixed mapping of restricted internal reasons to COMPLIANCE_REVIEW; free text gated by sponsor review.

**Business assumptions.**

- Sponsors accept a shared neutral code set.
- Restricted reasons (sanctions, investigations, law-enforcement requests) never surface in donor- or charity-facing text.

**Recommended answer.** Approve the 10-code catalog as sponsor-configurable defaults with: (1) donor-facing text that describes the policy outcome, not facts about a charity (e.g., PURPOSE_NOT_PERMITTED_BY_POLICY, not 'charity engaged in X'); (2) mandatory mapping of sanctions, investigation, and law-enforcement reasons to COMPLIANCE_REVIEW; (3) charity-facing communications limited to status codes; (4) free text optional, reviewed, and drawn from approved phrases; (5) an appeal/reconsideration path per UNB-XIN-CLM-001. This is a business policy; no legal reason-giving duty was found.

**Remaining uncertainty.** State consumer-protection theories (unfair practices) against opaque declines untested; counsel wording review still prudent but not a legal gate.

**Consequences if wrong.** Accusatory free text could create defamation claims by charities; revealing sanctions screening could compromise investigations.

**Conservative fallback.** Disable free text; show only the code label.

**Alternative business or architecture design.** Single generic 'Not approved' with private reason: lowest legal exposure, highest donor friction and support cost; loses actionable feedback.

**External authorization required.** none

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given a decline with an internal sanctions or investigation reason, Then the external code is COMPLIANCE_REVIEW and no internal reason text is exposed via UI, API, email or export.
- Given free text is entered, Then it cannot be released until a second reviewer approves it.
- Given a charity-facing message, Then it contains only status codes and no donor-identifying or investigation content.

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- Regulation requiring reason-giving for DAF declines
- Defamation claim or complaint involving decline text

**Related questions.**

- none

**Corrections to prior records.**

- XID-CLM-003 decision_outcome.authority ('NAIC Model 900 §4 (read by SVC pass)'): NAIC Model 900 governs insurance adverse underwriting decisions and does not apply to DAF sponsors; not verified in this pass. → Label it an analogy; the decision rests on sponsor discretion (program terms), not on any reason-giving duty.

### XID-EVA-004 — Confirm provisional decision: During a fund dispute freeze, do fee accruals, investment rebalancing and recurring grants continue?

**Disposition:** business-policy-decision · **Confidence:** high · **Jurisdiction:** Sponsor program terms; state UPMIFA (investment duties); federal §4966 · **Batch:** E (Governance, succession and delegated authority)

**Original provisional answer.** During a dispute freeze on a fund: fees continue to accrue; investments follow the standing investment policy but no new advisor-directed allocation changes are accepted; recurring and new grants are held; contributions may be accepted; nothing is distributed until the dispute is resolved by the sponsor.

**Relevant requirements.** UNB-XIN-EVA-010, ADM-023, /home/user/unbound/requirements/grants/END-TO-END-GRANT-LIFECYCLE-AND-OPERATING-MODEL.md, /home/user/unbound/requirements/administration/DAF-ADMINISTRATION-AND-SPONSOR-OPERATIONS.md

**Authoritative findings.**

- *established* — Sponsors may freeze accounts while account holders are under investigation or subject to court orders; terms do not specify fee or investment treatment during a freeze. [EV-E-003, Account privilege limitations; EV-E-002 Termination of account privileges]
- *established* — Fidelity's Trustees have exclusive ownership and control over all contributed property and earnings. [EV-E-001, Grants section closing paragraph]
- *established* — Institutional funds must be managed in good faith with ordinary prudence and diversified (UPMIFA). [EV-E-023, 13 MRSA §5103(2),(5)(D)]
- *interpretation* — Fees during a freeze are contractual; no law found requiring suspension of accruals. [EV-E-001, Fees and Expenses (absence)]

**Supporting interpretation.** An advisory dispute contests who may recommend, not the sponsor's ownership; the sponsor's investment duty continues, so investments should stay on the standing policy (including scheduled rebalancing) and fees accrue as the fund is still administered.

**Competing interpretation.** Recurring grants previously approved could be viewed as already-decided sponsor grants that should continue; but they derive from a contested advisor's standing instruction, so holding is safer. Fee waiver may be appropriate where the sponsor's error caused the dispute.

**Applicability to Unbound.** Implement a freeze matrix: accrual continues; collection continues; scheduled policy rebalancing continues; advisor-directed allocation changes blocked; recurring and new grants held; contributions accepted (with acknowledgment); outbound transfers held; mandatory/legal holds override.

**Business assumptions.**

- Sponsor investment policy defines automatic rebalancing independent of advisor instructions.
- Sponsors may waive fees case by case.

**Recommended answer.** Confirm: fees accrue and are collected (waiver is a separate sponsor decision); investments follow the standing investment policy including scheduled rebalancing; no advisor-directed allocation changes; recurring and new grants held; contributions accepted; no distribution or transfer until the sponsor resolves the dispute or a court orders otherwise.

**Remaining uncertainty.** Individual sponsor fee agreements may contain waiver commitments.

**Consequences if wrong.** Freezing investments could breach prudence duties; continuing recurring grants could pay contested instructions.

**Conservative fallback.** Hold all outflows; keep accruals; allow only policy rebalancing.

**Alternative business or architecture design.** Full freeze (no rebalancing, no accrual): simpler but risks prudence breach and revenue loss.

**External authorization required.** none

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given a DisputeFreeze, Then grant, recurring grant, transfer and allocation-change commands are rejected with DISPUTE_FREEZE and fee accrual and policy rebalancing jobs still run.
- Given a contribution arrives during a freeze, Then it is accepted and acknowledged and the freeze remains.
- Given a court order is recorded, Then it overrides the freeze matrix as directed.

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- Sponsor fee agreement changes
- Court order patterns

**Related questions.**

- **UNB-XIN-EVA-010#Q1**: No; fee accrual and collection continue; waiver is a separate sponsor decision recorded with reason.

**Corrections to prior records.**

- none

### XID-EVA-005 — Confirm provisional decision: Legal form of a sponsor-initiated bulk transfer of funds to another sponsor (grant to sponsoring organization vs asset transfer agreement) and ownership of historical re…

**Disposition:** reasoned-interpretation · **Confidence:** medium · **Jurisdiction:** US federal (§4966, Form 990 Schedules I/D/N); state nonprofit corporation/charitable trust law (AG notice for substantially-all-asset transfers, e.g. Cal. Corp. Code §5913); state privacy law for donor data · **Batch:** E (Governance, succession and delegated authority)

**Original provisional answer.** A sponsor-initiated bulk transfer is a series of per-fund grants to the receiving sponsoring organization (DAF_TRANSFER_OUT), under a master agreement that records each fund's advisory terms. The source sponsor keeps its records; the receiving sponsor gets a reference copy of fund history, with donor notice before personal data is transferred.

**Relevant requirements.** UNB-XIN-EVA-009, RR-UNB-WF-014-r1, UNB-RCN-ENT-007, /home/user/unbound/requirements/discovery/2026-11-23-cross-industry/data/decisions.csv

**Authoritative findings.**

- *established* — A distribution from a DAF to another DAF or to a 170(b)(1)(A) organization is not a taxable distribution, so per-fund grants to the receiving sponsor are permitted. [EV-E-005, §4966(c)(2)(A),(C)]
- *established* — Proposed regs define distribution broadly (any transfer, cash or in kind) and keep DAF-to-DAF non-taxable; still proposed as of 2026-11-24. [EV-E-007, Prop. §53.4966-5; EV-E-008]
- *established* — A transfer exceeding 25% of net assets (or a related series) that is not an ordinary-course grant must be reported on Schedule N Part II. [EV-E-020, Part II instructions, exclusions list]
- *established* — A California public benefit corporation must give the AG 20 days' notice before transferring all or substantially all assets outside the usual course. [EV-E-021, Cal. Corp. Code §5913]
- *established* — The source sponsor must retain books and records substantiating its receipts, disbursements and §6033 information. [EV-E-018, 26 CFR 1.6001-1(c),(e)]
- *interpretation* — Sponsor-initiated bulk transfers have precedent (BNY Mellon DAF program moved to RCF). [EV-E-025, P-NPT-03 section]

**Supporting interpretation.** Per-fund grants to the receiving sponsor keep each sponsor's books correct and use the statutory DAF-to-DAF exception; a master agreement documents the receiving sponsor's acceptance and expected advisory terms. The source sponsor keeps its records as custodian; the receiving sponsor needs history only as reference.

**Competing interpretation.** An assignment of the whole program (asset transfer agreement) could be cleaner for very large exits and may be required by state law where it is a substantially-all-asset disposition; it is still reportable on Schedule N. A master agreement that obliges the receiving sponsor to honor advisory privileges must not give donors legal control.

**Applicability to Unbound.** Unbound models DAF_TRANSFER_OUT per fund with a master-agreement reference, sponsor authorization separate from donor recommendations, and a size check against net assets (Schedule N flag) and state AG notice flags.

**Business assumptions.**

- Most transfers are well under 25% of the source sponsor's net assets.
- Donor privacy notices permit sharing fund history with a successor sponsor; otherwise notice/consent is obtained.

**Recommended answer.** Confirm: per-fund grants (DAF_TRANSFER_OUT) to the receiving sponsoring organization under a master agreement; each fund's transfer is a separate sponsor-authorized distribution with its own record; source sponsor remains records custodian; receiving sponsor gets a reference copy after donor notice. Add: (1) Schedule N flag if the transfer series exceeds 25% of the source's net assets; (2) state AG notice check where the transfer is all or substantially all assets (e.g., CA 20 days) or the sponsor is dissolving; (3) in-kind transfers allowed only where both sponsors agree (Fidelity has made in-kind DAF-to-DAF grants).

**Remaining uncertainty.** State-by-state AG notice/approval rules not surveyed beyond California; final §4966 regs pending.

**Consequences if wrong.** Missed AG notice or Schedule N reporting; disputes over records ownership.

**Conservative fallback.** Block execution until both sponsors' counsel approve the master agreement and any AG notice period expires.

**Alternative business or architecture design.** Whole-program asset transfer agreement: one legal instrument, needs AG review in many states; per-fund grants (recommended): fits existing grant controls and statute.

**External authorization required.** Executed master agreement between sponsors; board or delegated approval at the source sponsor; state AG notice/approval where the transfer is substantially all assets.

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given a bulk transfer, Then each fund produces a DAF_TRANSFER_OUT record with master-agreement ID and sponsor authorization distinct from any donor recommendation.
- Given the cumulative series exceeds 25% of the source sponsor's beginning net assets, Then a Schedule N flag is raised.
- Given the source sponsor is a California public benefit corporation transferring substantially all assets, Then execution is blocked until a 20-day AG notice is recorded.
- Given fund history is shared, Then donor notice is recorded before the export is released.

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- Final §4966 regulations
- Additional state AG requirements

**Related questions.**

- **UNB-XIN-EVA-009#Q1**: Per-fund grants to the receiving sponsor under a master agreement; sponsor authorization recorded separately from donor recommendations; execution blocked until the legal form, AG notice (if any) and history-sharing are approved.

**Corrections to prior records.**

- XID-EVA-005 decision_outcome.authority ('26 USC 4966(c)(2)'): Correct statute; precise basis is §4966(c)(2)(A) and (C); record omits Schedule N and state AG notice obligations for large transfers. → Cite (c)(2)(A),(C); add Schedule N >25% check and state AG notice gate.

### XID-REC-001 — Confirm provisional decision: After sponsor exit, how long does the platform keep read-only data and backups, and who is records custodian?

**Disposition:** business-policy-decision · **Confidence:** high · **Jurisdiction:** US federal (IRC §6001; Treas. Reg. §1.6001-1); state record-retention and privacy law; contract · **Batch:** E (Governance, succession and delegated authority)

**Original provisional answer.** After a sponsor exits, the sponsor is the records custodian. The platform keeps a read-only copy for 90 days after the reconciled export is delivered, then deletes it; backups expire on their normal schedule (no more than 35 days later). Legal holds override deletion. The exit certificate lists any residual copies and their expiry.

**Relevant requirements.** RR-UNB-TEN-016-r1, RCC-PLT-008, /home/user/unbound/requirements/discovery/2026-11-23-cross-industry/data/decisions.csv, /home/user/unbound/requirements/discovery/2026-11-23-cross-industry/16-LEGAL-AND-ACCOUNTING-DECISIONS.md

**Authoritative findings.**

- *established* — Exempt organizations must keep books showing receipts and disbursements and substantiating §6033 information, retained as long as material to tax administration. [EV-E-018, 26 CFR 1.6001-1(c),(e)]
- *established* — Generally keep records until the statute of limitations runs (3 years after the later of due date or filing), longer for other legal purposes; governance and exemption documents permanently. [EV-E-019, Pub 4221-PC p.14]
- *interpretation* — The retention duty rests on the exempt organization (sponsor), not on its software provider. [EV-E-018, §1.6001-1(c)]

**Supporting interpretation.** Since the sponsor bears the duty, the sponsor is the custodian; a platform residual copy is a contractual courtesy for export verification. 90 days of read-only access after a verified export is reasonable.

**Competing interpretation.** Some sponsors may want the platform to hold records for 7 years as an outsourced archive (contractual service); the platform then becomes a records processor with longer obligations.

**Applicability to Unbound.** Offer exit export with checksum verification, 90-day read-only period, backup expiry ≤35 days after deletion, legal holds override, exit certificate listing residuals. Optionally offer paid long-term archive.

**Business assumptions.**

- Sponsor accepts custody of a complete, verified export.
- Backups rotate on a ≤35-day schedule.

**Recommended answer.** Confirm: sponsor is records custodian; platform keeps read-only copy 90 days after the sponsor confirms the reconciled export, then deletes; backups expire ≤35 days later; legal holds override; exit certificate lists residuals. Contract must state that the sponsor is responsible for retaining records for at least the federal limitation period (generally 3 years after the later of the 990 due/filing date; longer for permanent records and state requirements) and that the platform's optional archive service is separate.

**Remaining uncertainty.** State-specific retention laws and privacy deletion rights not surveyed.

**Consequences if wrong.** Sponsor unable to substantiate returns if export incomplete; platform liability if data retained beyond contract.

**Conservative fallback.** Hold read-only until the sponsor signs export acceptance, however long that takes, with a contractual fee after 90 days.

**Alternative business or architecture design.** Paid 7-year archive service: revenue opportunity, longer data exposure.

**External authorization required.** Executed sponsor contract clause on custody, retention and exit.

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given a sponsor exits, When the reconciled export is accepted, Then deletion is scheduled for 90 days later and the exit certificate lists backup expiry dates.
- Given a legal hold exists, Then deletion is blocked and the certificate reflects the hold.

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- New state retention law
- Sponsor demand for archive service

**Related questions.**

- **RR-UNB-TEN-016-r1#Q1**: Complete verified export; 90-day read-only period at platform cost (longer at sponsor cost); mandatory retention and legal holds override deletion; sponsor retains records per IRC §6001/Reg. §1.6001-1.

**Corrections to prior records.**

- XID-REC-001 decision_outcome.authority (RR-UNB-TEN-016-r1; RCC-PLT-008): Cites only internal records; no legal basis. → Add 26 USC 6001 and Treas. Reg. §1.6001-1(c),(e); IRS Pub 4221-PC retention guidance.

### XID-SAS-002 — Confirm provisional decision: In a distribution-partner exit, may the original sponsor approve outbound transfer grants by a batch policy, and may it decline or impose conditions?

**Disposition:** reasoned-interpretation · **Confidence:** medium · **Jurisdiction:** US federal (§4966); sponsor program terms; state charity law for large asset transfers · **Batch:** E (Governance, succession and delegated authority)

**Original provisional answer.** In a partner exit the original sponsor may adopt a written exit policy as the exercise of its discretion. The policy is applied per fund: each transfer is a separate decision with its own record citing the policy, hold and sanctions checks. The sponsor may decline or condition individual transfers (open holds, sanctions matches, expenditure-responsibility grants outstanding, unresolved disputes). A contractual pre-commitment to transfer that removes sponsor discretion is not permitted.

**Relevant requirements.** UNB-XIN-SAS-007, RR-UNB-WF-014-r1, UNB-XIN-EVA-009, UNB-XIN-EVA-011, XIC-ADV-008, /home/user/unbound/requirements/discovery/2026-11-23-cross-industry/data/decisions.csv, /home/user/unbound/requirements/discovery/2026-11-23-cross-industry/16-LEGAL-AND-ACCOUNTING-DECISIONS.md

**Authoritative findings.**

- *established* — A distribution from a DAF to any other DAF, or to a 170(b)(1)(A) public charity (including another sponsor), is not a taxable distribution. [EV-E-005, §4966(c)(2)(A),(C)]
- *interpretation* — The DAF is owned and controlled by the sponsor; a binding pre-commitment to transfer could undercut 'owned and controlled' (interpretation). [EV-E-005, §4966(d)(2)(A)(ii)]
- *established* — Proposed regs would treat a series of distributions under a plan inconsistent with §4966 as one distribution - proposed only. [EV-E-007, Prop. §53.4966-5 special rule; EV-E-008 status]
- *established* — Fidelity acknowledges grants to other DAF sponsors (excluded from its activity test); NCF bars succession funds from transferring to other sponsors; no sponsor publishes an outbound-transfer procedure or fee. [EV-E-001, Minimum grant requirements; EV-E-025 P-QA3-02]
- *established* — Transfers over 25% of net assets that are not ordinary-course grants are reported on Schedule N Part II. [EV-E-020, Part II instructions]

**Supporting interpretation.** A written exit policy adopted by the sponsor's board or authorized officer is itself an exercise of the sponsor's discretion; applying it per fund with individual decision records preserves control and creates an audit trail. Declining or conditioning transfers for open holds, sanctions, ER grants outstanding, or disputes is within sponsor discretion.

**Competing interpretation.** If the partner agreement obliges the sponsor to transfer on exit, the commitment may be read as donor or third-party control over DAF assets; also, fees or delays imposed to discourage exit are lawful contractually but may attract reputational/regulatory scrutiny.

**Applicability to Unbound.** Unbound generates batch recommendations and per-fund decision records; executes only after a recorded sponsor approval per fund (or per policy with each fund's checks passed and logged).

**Business assumptions.**

- Distribution-partner agreements will contain no mandatory transfer clause.
- Receiving sponsors are verified 170(b)(1)(A) organizations.

**Recommended answer.** Confirm: sponsor adopts a written exit policy (by its board or delegated officer) as its discretionary decision; Unbound applies it fund by fund, each with its own record citing the policy, eligibility, sanctions and hold checks; sponsor may decline or condition individual transfers; no contractual pre-commitment that removes discretion. Fees and deadlines: lawful if disclosed in program terms; no legal restriction found, but avoid terms that make transfer effectively impossible. If the exit moves >25% of sponsor net assets, flag Schedule N reporting.

**Remaining uncertainty.** No IRS guidance on batch-policy approvals; final §4966 regulations could add anti-abuse rules.

**Consequences if wrong.** Loss of exclusive-control posture; disputes with exiting partners.

**Conservative fallback.** Per-fund manual approval by a sponsor officer.

**Alternative business or architecture design.** Per-fund manual approval: zero ambiguity, high labor (minutes per fund x thousands). Batch policy + per-fund automated checks + officer sign-off of the batch list (recommended): low labor, same control evidence.

**External authorization required.** Sponsor board resolution or delegated-authority approval adopting the exit policy.

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given an exit policy is approved, When a batch runs, Then each fund produces an individual decision record with policy ID, checks and outcome.
- Given a fund has an open hold, sanctions match, outstanding ER grant or dispute, Then it is excluded from the batch and routed for individual decision.
- Given a partner contract contains a mandatory-transfer clause, Then contract onboarding is blocked pending sponsor legal review.

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- Final §4966 regulations
- IRS guidance on DAF-to-DAF transfers

**Related questions.**

- **UNB-XIN-SAS-007#Q1**: Yes, by a written sponsor-adopted exit policy applied per fund with individual decision records; batch recommendation is deterministic; execution only with recorded sponsor authority.
- **UNB-XIN-SAS-007#Q2**: No statute found restricting fees or timing; sponsor may impose disclosed contractual fees and deadlines but should not make lawful transfer impracticable; disclose in program terms.

**Corrections to prior records.**

- XID-SAS-002 decision_outcome.authority ('IRC 4966(d)(2)(A)(ii)'): Correct but incomplete; the permitted-distribution basis is §4966(c)(2)(A)/(C). → Add §4966(c)(2)(A) and (C) and note Schedule N for large exits.

### XID-TRS-001 — Confirm provisional decision: Can an agent under POA, guardian or conservator exercise DAF advisory privileges?

**Disposition:** reasoned-interpretation · **Confidence:** medium · **Jurisdiction:** State law (UPOAA in roughly 26-30 states; non-uniform POA statutes elsewhere, e.g. FL, NY, CA); state guardianship/conservatorship law (UGCOPAA enactments, older UGPPA); federal §4966 for DAF definition · **Batch:** E (Governance, succession and delegated authority)

**Original provisional answer.** An agent under a power of attorney may exercise advisory privileges only if the instrument expressly grants authority to make gifts or charitable donations, the sponsor's program terms allow representatives, and the instrument is verified. Guardians and conservators act only under a court order that covers charitable gifts. Otherwise the privileges are suspended pending succession or court order.

**Relevant requirements.** UNB-XIN-TRS-004, REQ-X-068, /home/user/unbound/requirements/discovery/2026-11-23-cross-industry/data/decisions.csv, /home/user/unbound/requirements/discovery/2026-11-24-final-55-question-resolutions.md

**Authoritative findings.**

- *established* — UPOAA §201(a): an agent may make a gift on behalf of the principal or with the principal's property only if the POA expressly grants that authority AND exercise is not otherwise prohibited by another agreement or instrument to which the authority or property is subject. [EV-E-009, UPOAA §201(a)(2) and lead-in]
- *established* — UPOAA §120: a person presented with an acknowledged POA must accept it or request certification/translation/opinion within seven business days unless a refusal ground applies (e.g., not otherwise required to transact with the principal in the same circumstances; good-faith belief agent lacks authority; APS report); wrongful refusal risks a court order and fees. [EV-E-009, UPOAA §120(a)-(c)]
- *established* — NC enactment varies the uniform text: general gift authority reaches charitable gifts only 'in accordance with the principal's history of making or joining in the making of gifts'. [EV-E-010, G.S. 32C-2-217(b)(1)b]
- *established* — Conservators need specific court authorization, after notice, to make non-de-minimis gifts (UGCOPAA enactment, Maine). [EV-E-012, 18-C MRSA §5-414(1)(A)]
- *interpretation* — DAF assets are owned and controlled by the sponsor; advisory privileges arise from donor status or donor appointment, so a DAF grant recommendation is not a gift of the principal's property. [EV-E-005, §4966(d)(2)(A)]
- *established* — Vanguard accepts a copy of a durable POA as written proof of the advisor's incapacity, which triggers the succession plan rather than conferring privileges on the agent. [EV-E-002, Account structure 'Successor advisor'; Enacting a succession plan]
- *established* — DAFgiving360: an executor or trustee cannot exercise account privileges unless also a named successor; Fidelity: only Account Holders and Authorized Persons designated by an Account Holder exercise privileges, and Fidelity may decline or revoke any designation. [EV-E-003, p.31 n.14; EV-E-001 'Fidelity Charitable approval', 'Authorized Person']
- *established* — Proposed regs would treat any person appointed or designated by a donor, with no particular form, as a donor-advisor (relevant to §4967/§4958 status of agents) - proposed only, not effective as of 2026-11-24. [EV-E-007, Prop. §53.4966-1(h)(1); EV-E-008]

**Supporting interpretation.** Because the sponsor owns the assets and the privilege is contractual, the governing question is what the program terms allow. UPOAA §201(a) itself defers to 'another agreement ... to which the authority ... is subject', so sponsor terms that limit representative authority are effective in UPOAA states. Requiring express gift/charitable authority is a sound proxy for the principal's intent, but the strictly legal hook is the program terms. A sponsor that never accepts agents (only donor-designated Authorized Persons) is consistent with Fidelity, DAFgiving360 and Vanguard practice.

**Competing interpretation.** An agent with broad general authority could argue that recommending grants from a DAF is not a 'gift' (the gift was made at contribution) and so does not need express gift authority; and UPOAA §120 could be read to require the sponsor to accept an acknowledged POA within 7 business days. Counter: §120(b)(1) excuses acceptance where the person is not otherwise required to transact with the principal in the same circumstances; the sponsor never must act on a recommendation. The sponsor's terms should say expressly that agents act only as the program allows. States outside UPOAA (e.g., Florida §709.2202 requires principal's signature/initials next to gift powers - recalled, not fetched) can be stricter.

**Applicability to Unbound.** Unbound should implement a representative-authority record (instrument type, jurisdiction, acknowledgment, scope flags incl. express gift/charitable authority, court order scope, expiry) and a 7-business-day POA response clock with statutory refusal-reason codes, while the sponsor's terms decide whether agents may act at all.

**Business assumptions.**

- Sponsor program terms will state whether agents, guardians and conservators may exercise privileges.
- Sponsors will want to avoid UPOAA §120 refusal liability by responding within 7 business days with a documented ground.

**Recommended answer.** Confirm with refinements: (1) Default: an agent under a POA may exercise advisory privileges only if (a) the sponsor's program terms permit representatives, (b) the instrument is acknowledged and verified, and (c) it expressly grants authority to make gifts or charitable donations (or expressly references the DAF/advisory privileges); exercise must follow the principal's giving history and may not benefit the agent. Contributions by an agent always require express gift authority (UPOAA §§201, 217). (2) Guardians of the person have no authority; conservators act only under a court order specifically authorizing charitable gifts or DAF advisory acts. (3) Otherwise treat the POA/court order as evidence of incapacity, suspend the principal's privileges, and activate the next named advisor/successor per program terms (Vanguard pattern). (4) Respond to any presented POA within 7 business days (accept, request certification, or refuse with a §120 ground) and log it. (5) The agent's acts are logged as the agent's, and the agent is treated as a donor-advisor for impermissible-benefit screening.

**Remaining uncertainty.** No case law or IRS guidance found on agents exercising DAF advisory privileges; state variations (non-UPOAA states, NC-style charitable-history limits, Florida execution formalities) not fully surveyed.

**Consequences if wrong.** Accepting an agent without authority could facilitate elder exploitation and expose the sponsor to claims; refusing without a statutory ground in a UPOAA state could bring a court order and fee award.

**Conservative fallback.** Program terms state that representatives may not exercise advisory privileges; POAs and court orders are used only to verify incapacity and trigger succession; respond within 7 business days citing §120(b)(1)-type ground.

**Alternative business or architecture design.** (A) Donor-designated Authorized Person/interested-party roles set while the donor has capacity (Fidelity/Vanguard pattern) instead of accepting POAs: lowest exposure, modest friction (donor must act in advance), small lost opportunity for incapacitated donors without designees. (B) Accept agents with express authority (recommended default): medium exposure, needs instrument review (~30 min per case). (C) Accept any general POA: highest exposure; rejected.

**External authorization required.** Sponsor program-terms clause on representatives approved by the sponsor; for conservators, a court order specifically authorizing the act.

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given an acknowledged POA is uploaded, When 7 business days elapse without a decision, Then the case escalates and the clock state is visible to the sponsor.
- Given a POA without express gift or charitable authority, When the agent recommends a grant, Then the recommendation is blocked with reason code REPRESENTATIVE_AUTHORITY_INSUFFICIENT.
- Given a conservator presents letters without a court order authorizing gifts, Then advisory privileges are suspended and succession/next-advisor routing is offered.
- Given an agent-recommended grant would benefit the agent or their dependants, Then it is blocked and routed to compliance.
- Given sponsor terms disallow representatives, When any POA is presented, Then the platform records a refusal citing the program-terms ground within 7 business days.

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- Final §4966 regulations (donor-advisor definition)
- Court decision on agents and DAF privileges
- Sponsor operates in a non-UPOAA state with mandatory acceptance rules
- SR-style statutory changes to POA acceptance periods

**Related questions.**

- **UNB-XIN-TRS-004#Q1**: Only where sponsor terms allow representatives and a verified, acknowledged instrument expressly grants gift/charitable authority; record instrument, scope, expiry and sponsor acceptance; a generic POA confers no authority; respond within 7 business days (UPOAA §120).

**Corrections to prior records.**

- XID-TRS-001 decision_outcome.authority ('Uniform Power of Attorney Act §201 (general knowledge)'): Cited from general knowledge; also omits that §201(a) defers to other agreements governing the authority and omits the §120 seven-business-day acceptance duty. → Cite UPOAA §§119, 120, 201(a), 217 (fetched) and state enactments; add 7-business-day response clock and refusal-ground logging.
- XID-TRS-001 rationale: Implies the gift-authority rule directly governs DAF grant recommendations; DAF assets are sponsor-owned, so the controlling rule is the sponsor's program terms (interpretation). → State that program terms govern; express gift authority is the required evidential threshold, mandatory for contributions by agents.

### RCD-007 — Confirm provisional decision: Approve the retention class catalog, including OFAC 10 years and the 7-year platform floor.

**Disposition:** established-by-authoritative-evidence · **Confidence:** high · **Jurisdiction:** US-federal (OFAC, IRS), ACH network rules (Nacha), US states (records, charitable solicitation, unclaimed property) · **Batch:** F (Privacy, communications and sanctions)

**Original provisional answer.** Adopt the authority-keyed retention catalog: 7-year platform floor (policy); OFAC records 10 years; basis and carryover records 7 years after final disposition; reliance snapshots at least the floor. Disposition runs as approved class-level batches; no automatic deletion.

**Relevant requirements.** RCF-REG-003, RCF-PLT-013, RCF-PLT-014, RCF-FIN-035, REQ-X-036, RR-REQ-X-036-r1, requirements/reconciliation/2026-11-23/data/decisions.csv, requirements/discovery/2026-11-24-simplification-economics/18-REFINED-REQUIREMENTS.md

**Authoritative findings.**

- *established* — OFAC recordkeeping is 10 years after the transaction date for any transaction subject to 31 CFR chapter V, and for blocked property the period lasts while blocked plus at least 10 years after unblocking. [EV-F-001, 31 CFR 501.601]
- *established* — The 5-to-10-year change was made by an interim final rule (89 FR 74832, Sept 13, 2024) effective March 12, 2025, adopted as final without change effective March 21, 2025. [EV-F-004, 90 FR 13286 Summary, Dates, Background]
- *established* — eCFR shows 501.601 substantive versions dated 2024-09-13 and 2025-03-12; the current text is the 10-year text. [EV-F-005, versioner history for 501.601]
- *established* — The change implements Pub. L. 118-50 div. D s.3111 (10-year IEEPA/TWEA limitations period, signed April 24, 2024). [EV-F-037, as described at 90 FR 13286]
- *established* — IRS: records must be kept as long as material to tax administration; generally until the return's limitations period runs (3 years after due/filed date), permanent retention for exemption application, determination letter, organizing documents and board minutes, employment tax records at least 4 years. [EV-F-014, Pub. 4221-PC p.18]
- *established* — Treasury regulation standard: retain so long as the contents may become material. [EV-F-013, 26 CFR 1.6001-1(e)]
- *interpretation* — Nacha consumer debit authorizations: retain two years from termination or revocation (secondary sources, rule 2.3.2.7; rulebook not fetched). [EV-F-015, secondary reports]
- *established* — OFAC expects recordkeeping procedures to account for OFAC requirements as an internal-control element. [EV-F-006, Framework p.5 Internal Controls IV]

**Supporting interpretation.** The OFAC 10-year period is established law, not a policy choice. Because 501.601 reaches 'every person engaging in any transaction subject to' the chapter, and nearly every contribution and grant a US sponsor makes is a transaction by a US person, the safe reading (interpretation) is that all contribution, grant-payment and screening/disposition records fall in the OFAC class. A 7-year platform floor is a sound policy: it covers the IRS 3-year general period, the 6-year substantial-omission period (not fetched) and Nacha's 2 years.

**Competing interpretation.** A narrower reading would limit 501.601 to transactions involving sanctioned persons, programs or licenses. OFAC's 2025 final rule noted a commenter asked for applicability guidance and gave none. Relying on the narrow reading saves little storage and adds classification risk.

**Applicability to Unbound.** Sponsors are the US persons engaging in the transactions and are the primary recordkeepers. Unbound holds the records as the sponsor's processor/agent, so the catalog must let Unbound keep sponsor records for 10 years and must stop Unbound from deleting a sponsor's OFAC-class records when a contract ends without first exporting them to the sponsor.

**Business assumptions.**

- Sponsors are US 501(c)(3) public charities subject to OFAC.
- Unbound stores sponsor records as a service provider under contract.
- Storage cost of 10-year retention is small relative to classification and deletion-review labour.

**Recommended answer.** Confirm the catalog with these refinements: (1) OFAC class = 10 years after the transaction date (contribution receipt, grant payment, rejected or declined sanctioned transaction), and for blocked property = while blocked + 10 years after unblocking. Include screening results, potential-match dispositions and license reliance records tied to those transactions (interpretation, conservative). (2) Keep the 7-year platform floor as policy for other financial and grant records. Simpler alternative: apply 10 years to all transaction-level financial records, so the system does not have to classify which records are OFAC records. (3) Permanent class for sponsor organizing documents, determination letters and board/committee minutes that Unbound hosts. (4) Nacha authorization records = max(2 years after termination/revocation, floor). (5) Legal hold overrides all disposition. (6) Disposition through approved class-level batches with no per-record review. Keep the existing 'no automatic deletion without batch approval' rule.

**Remaining uncertainty.** Scope of 'transaction subject to the provisions of this chapter' is not defined by OFAC guidance; state-specific record laws (e.g. charitable solicitation registration records, unclaimed property records) not catalogued here; Nacha rule text not fetched.

**Consequences if wrong.** If OFAC records were deleted before 10 years, the sponsor could be unable to defend itself during the 10-year limitations period, and OFAC treats missing records as a recordkeeping violation (penalties under the IEEPA/TWEA frameworks). Over-retention mainly adds privacy exposure and storage cost.

**Conservative fallback.** Single 10-year minimum for all transaction-level and screening records; permanent for governance records; holds override.

**Alternative business or architecture design.** Alternative A (recommended simplification): one 10-year floor for all financial/transaction and screening records. Cost: modest storage. Friction: none. Exposure: lowest. Lost opportunity: earlier privacy minimisation for non-OFAC records. Alternative B (as provisionally decided): 7-year floor plus a 10-year OFAC class. Cost: classification logic and a risk of misclassification. Exposure: low if classification is right. Alternative C: indefinite retention. Cost: privacy/data-minimisation exposure (state privacy laws, EU donors); rejected.

**External authorization required.** Sponsor compliance owner approval of the retention schedule (an internal policy approval, not counsel opinion); sponsor contract clause assigning record custody on termination.

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given a grant payment dated D, when the disposition batch runs on any date before D+10 years, then the payment record and its screening evidence are excluded from the batch.
- Given blocked property unblocked on date U, when disposition runs before U+10 years, then all related records are excluded.
- Given a record class with a legal hold, when any disposition batch is proposed, then the held records are excluded and the batch shows the hold ID.
- Given a sponsor contract terminates, when offboarding runs, then OFAC-class records are exported to the sponsor with a manifest before any Unbound-side deletion and deletion requires a sponsor-signed release.
- Given an ACH debit authorization revoked on R, when disposition runs, then the authorization is retained until max(R+2 years, platform floor).

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- OFAC issues guidance narrowing or widening 501.601 scope
- Congress changes IEEPA/TWEA limitations period
- Nacha amends authorization retention
- A target state enacts a longer charity-records requirement
- Privacy regulator action on over-retention

**Related questions.**

- none

**Corrections to prior records.**

- Catalog text 'OFAC/sanctions records (10 years from the transaction or screening decision, 31 CFR 501...)': The regulation measures the period from the transaction date (and, for blocked property, from the unblocking date), not from the screening decision. → Start the 10-year period at the transaction date, or at the unblocking date for blocked property. Screening records inherit the period of the transaction they relate to. A screening with no transaction keeps the platform floor, or 10 years under the conservative option.
- RCD-007 validation_required 'Counsel Q5': The 10-year OFAC period is directly verifiable in the eCFR and the 2025 final rule; it does not need a counsel opinion. → Mark the OFAC period as established (31 CFR 501.601; 90 FR 13286). Only the policy floor and state additions remain sponsor policy decisions.

### RCD-015 — Confirm provisional decision: Sanctions: screening scope, true-match blocking and OFAC reporting.

**Disposition:** reasoned-interpretation · **Confidence:** high · **Jurisdiction:** US-federal (OFAC) · **Batch:** F (Privacy, communications and sanctions)

**Original provisional answer.** Adopt UNB-RCN-GRT-006/007 and UNB-RCN-REG-003. Screen donors, advisors, successors, recipient entities, payee account holders and the recipient's officers where data is available, at onboarding, at grant approval, at payment release and on every list update. Potential matches are held; humans disposition them; a confirmed match leads to Blocked and OFAC reporting.

**Relevant requirements.** RCF-GRT-013, RCF-GRT-014, RCF-REG-006, UNB-RCN-GRT-006, UNB-RCN-GRT-007, UNB-RCN-REG-003, RR-UNB-EXT-004-r1, CTL-013, SPW-004, SPW-085

**Authoritative findings.**

- *established* — No OFAC regulation prescribes a screening scope or tool; OFAC strongly encourages a risk-based program with five components, whose risk assessment covers customers, supply chain, intermediaries and counterparties. [EV-F-006, Framework p.1, p.3]
- *established* — Screening tools must be updated for list changes, account for alternative spellings and be tested. [EV-F-006, Framework p.5 II; p.10 root cause VI]
- *established* — Potential matches are evaluated by comparing identifiers; many alerts are false positives; after a likely match the organization checks licenses/exemptions and then decides to block, reject or proceed, documents the investigation and reports within 10 business days; OFAC does not confirm matches. [EV-F-008, FAQ 5 steps 3-6]
- *established* — All U.S. persons must comply with OFAC sanctions. [EV-F-009, FAQ 11]
- *established* — Holders of blocked property report within 10 business days and file annual reports of property held at June 30 by September 30. [EV-F-002, 501.603(b)(1)(i), (b)(2)(i)]
- *established* — Rejected (non-blocked but prohibited) transactions are reported within 10 business days. [EV-F-003, 501.604(a)(1), (c)]
- *established* — Blocking applies when a blocked person has an interest in property; rejection when no blockable interest exists; a declined inquiry creates nothing to reject. [EV-F-007, FAQ 36, 42, 53]

**Supporting interpretation.** The provisional scope (donors, advisors, successors, recipient entities, payee account holders, recipient officers where available; screened at onboarding, approval, release and on list updates) matches the Framework's counterparty risk assessment and is defensible. Holding potential matches for human disposition matches FAQ 5. Interpretation: when a donor, advisor or successor is a confirmed SDN, their advisory privileges and any succession rights are probably an 'interest' of a blocked person (OFAC reads 'interest' broadly), so the safe course is to block the account's advisory privileges and report it, while the sponsor keeps legal title to the fund.

**Competing interpretation.** Some sponsors screen only donors and grantee organizations and not grantee officers, citing data unavailability. That is permissible under a risk-based program but leaves exposure for small foreign recipients. The OFAC 50 Percent Rule (entities owned 50% or more by blocked persons are blocked) is not covered by name screening alone (not fetched here; from general knowledge).

**Applicability to Unbound.** Unbound supplies screening and workflow; the sponsor is the US person with blocking/reporting duties and must own dispositions. Unbound can prepare ORS-ready report data but filing is by or on behalf of the sponsor (agents may file; primary responsibility stays with the holder).

**Business assumptions.**

- Sponsors accept risk-based scope documented in a ScreeningScope matrix.
- Screening vendor list coverage includes SDN and non-SDN consolidated lists.

**Recommended answer.** Confirm with two refinements. (1) A confirmed true match does not automatically mean 'Blocked'. Use the FAQ 5 step-5 three-way outcome: Block when a blocked person has an interest in property in the sponsor's possession or control (for example an SDN donor/advisor's fund privileges, or funds already in transit to an SDN), Reject when the transaction is prohibited but nothing is blockable, and Proceed when a license or exemption applies. Reports go within 10 business days of the block or reject action, and blocked property is added to the annual June 30/September 30 report. (2) Re-screening on list updates should be delta-based and automatic, and every disposition must be documented with the identifiers compared (FAQ 5 step 6). Keep the scope matrix as a sponsor-approved risk assessment artifact.

**Remaining uncertainty.** Exact treatment of DAF advisory privileges as blocked 'interests' has no OFAC guidance located; 50 Percent Rule coverage depends on ownership data availability.

**Consequences if wrong.** Missing a true match risks strict-liability civil penalties. Wrongly blocking or rejecting causes donor harm and reputational cost but carries little legal exposure.

**Conservative fallback.** Block and freeze all activity on any confirmed match involving a donor/advisor/successor; reject any grant to a confirmed-match recipient; prepare report data for both within 10 business days for compliance decision to file.

**Alternative business or architecture design.** Alternative: restrict R1 grants to US IRS-listed 501(c)(3) public charities (domestic-only). Cost: lost international grantmaking. Friction: low. Exposure: sharply reduced (US charities rarely SDN; screening still needed). Alternative: outsource screening/disposition to sponsor's bank or vendor. Cost: vendor fees; exposure unchanged since liability stays with sponsor.

**External authorization required.** Sponsor compliance officer designation and sponsor approval of the ScreeningScope matrix (internal governance).

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given a list update adds an entry similar to an existing donor, when the delta rescreen runs, then a potential-match case opens within 24 hours and any pending grant from that fund is held.
- Given a confirmed match where the blocked person is the donor/advisor, when the officer confirms, then advisory privileges are frozen, the status becomes Blocked, and an ORS-ready initial blocking report draft with a due date 10 business days later is created.
- Given a confirmed match on a grant recipient with no funds yet transferred, when the officer confirms, then the grant is stopped and the disposition (reject-report or no-report) is recorded with rationale.
- Given blocked property exists on June 30, when July 1 arrives, then an annual report task due September 30 is created.
- Given any disposition, when saved, then compared identifiers, list entry version and officer identity are stored and retained 10 years.

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- OFAC guidance on DAFs or charitable intermediaries
- Changes to FAQ 5/36/53
- New 50 Percent Rule guidance
- Expansion to non-US recipients beyond R1 scope

**Related questions.**

- none

**Corrections to prior records.**

- RCD-015 decided_option 'a confirmed match leads to Blocked and OFAC reporting': This treats every confirmed match as a blocking. OFAC distinguishes blocking from rejection from proceeding under a license (FAQ 5 step 5; FAQ 36). → Replace it with a three-way outcome (Block/Reject/Proceed-under-license) and keep the reporting duty specific to each action.

### RCD-044 — Confirm provisional decision: True sanctions match before grant approval

**Disposition:** reasoned-interpretation · **Confidence:** medium · **Jurisdiction:** US-federal (OFAC) · **Batch:** F (Privacy, communications and sanctions)

**Original provisional answer.** Refuse the grant, release any reservation, produce OFAC rejected-transaction report data within 10 business days, and show the donor a neutral message.

**Relevant requirements.** UNB-RCN-WF-024, RCF-WF-003, UNB-RCN-REG-003

**Authoritative findings.**

- *established* — When a customer merely asks whether a prohibited transfer can be made and the bank declines, 'there is nothing to reject' and no reject report is needed; once payment instructions are received the bank must reject (and report) or block. [EV-F-007, FAQ 53; FAQ 42]
- *established* — Rejected transactions must not be processed forward; rejected transactions are reported within 10 business days. [EV-F-007, FAQ 36; FAQ 49]
- *established* — A reject report is required where a US person rejects a transaction that is not blocked but would violate OFAC rules. [EV-F-003, 501.604(a)(1)]
- *established* — Blocked property must be reported within 10 business days of blocking. [EV-F-002, 501.603(b)(1)(i)]
- *established* — Records of the investigation must be kept; OFAC may request them. [EV-F-008, FAQ 5 step 6]

**Supporting interpretation.** Interpretation: a donor's grant recommendation is advice the sponsor may decline, which makes it close to FAQ 53's customer inquiry. If the sponsor declines before approval and before any payment instruction goes to a bank or rail, no transaction was processed or rejected and 501.604 is probably not triggered. The reservation is an internal earmark of the sponsor's own funds, in which the SDN recipient has no interest, so releasing it is lawful, provided the donor/advisor is not also a blocked person.

**Competing interpretation.** A conservative reading treats the sponsor's refusal of a grant to an SDN as 'rejecting a transaction' that would violate OFAC rules, which would require a 501.604 report. Filing a precautionary report costs little and protects the sponsor.

**Applicability to Unbound.** The sponsor decides and files. Unbound must keep three states distinct (internal refusal, rejected transaction and blocked property) and must not auto-release a reservation if any blocked person (including the donor/advisor) has an interest.

**Business assumptions.**

- Grant approval precedes any payment instruction to a bank or payment rail.
- Sponsor retains legal ownership of DAF assets.

**Recommended answer.** Revise. On a true recipient match before approval: (1) refuse the recommendation and never process it forward. (2) Release the reservation only if the donor, advisor and successors screen clear; otherwise freeze the fund (see RCD-015). (3) Always assemble 501.604-format report data and open a compliance task due 10 business days from refusal. The sponsor compliance officer records 'no report: declined request before instruction (FAQ 53 analogue)' or files a reject report; the default recommendation is to file, because a precautionary report is low-cost. (4) After approval, or once a payment instruction exists, the case is a reject (or a block if the funds are already in the recipient's interest) with a mandatory report within 10 business days. (5) Show the donor a neutral message. No OFAC rule prohibiting disclosure was found, but neutral wording limits evasion.

**Remaining uncertainty.** No OFAC guidance addresses DAF recommendations specifically; FAQ 53 is a bank-customer analogy.

**Consequences if wrong.** If a report was required and not filed, the result is a reporting violation (generally a non-egregious penalty) and loss of mitigation credit. If the reservation is wrongly released while the donor is blocked, the sponsor has dealt in blocked property.

**Conservative fallback.** File a reject report within 10 business days for every true recipient match regardless of stage; hold reservation pending compliance confirmation that no blocked person has an interest.

**Alternative business or architecture design.** Avoidance design: screen recipients when the recommendation is drafted, before submission, so most SDN recipients are blocked from selection and no recommendation is ever made. Cost: an extra screening call. Friction: none. Exposure: lower. Lost opportunity: none.

**External authorization required.** none

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given a recommendation to a confirmed SDN recipient before approval, when the officer confirms the match, then the grant status becomes Refused-Sanctions, no payment instruction can be created, and a report-decision task due in 10 business days is opened with prefilled 501.604 data.
- Given the same case where the donor screens clear, when refusal is recorded, then the reservation is released and the release reason references the clear donor screen.
- Given a confirmed match after a payment instruction exists, when confirmed, then report filing is mandatory (no 'no report' option) and the payment is cancelled or recalled or marked blocked.
- Given any refusal, when the donor views the grant, then a neutral message appears without naming the list.

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- OFAC FAQ or enforcement action addressing grantmakers or DAFs
- Sponsor counsel policy on precautionary filing

**Related questions.**

- none

**Corrections to prior records.**

- UNB-RCN-WF-024 basis 'RCD-044; 31 CFR 501.604 (unverified - general knowledge)': 501.604 is now verified, but the record presumes that a reject report is always required and that the reservation is always released. → Cite 501.604(a)(1),(c) and OFAC FAQ 36/53 as verified. Report decision is made by the sponsor, with a precautionary-filing default. Release the reservation only after a clear donor/advisor screen.

### SPD-ADV-002 — May a sanctions false-positive disposition made by one sponsor's authorised officer be relied on by another sponsor's screening (RR-UNB-EXT-004-r2)?

**Disposition:** business-policy-decision · **Confidence:** medium · **Jurisdiction:** US-federal (OFAC) · **Batch:** F (Privacy, communications and sanctions)

**Original provisional answer.** Recommendation: No by default: reuse scoped to the disposing sponsor legal entity; cross-sponsor reliance only with counsel approval, a recorded reliance event per relying sponsor, and invalidation propagated to all relying sponsors.

**Relevant requirements.** SPD-ADV-002, RR-UNB-EXT-004-r2

**Authoritative findings.**

- *established* — All U.S. persons must comply; each sponsor is separately responsible. [EV-F-009, FAQ 11]
- *established* — OFAC does not confirm false positives; each organization documents its own analysis. [EV-F-008, FAQ 5]
- *established* — Where IT solutions are used, the organization must select, calibrate and test them for its own risk profile. [EV-F-006, Framework p.5 II]

**Supporting interpretation.** OFAC does not prohibit relying on another party's screening work, but reliance does not transfer liability. Cross-sponsor reuse is therefore permissible as an internal-control choice if each relying sponsor adopts it and can evidence the underlying analysis.

**Competing interpretation.** Sharing one sponsor's donor-related dispositions with another sponsor may disclose confidential donor information, so privacy/contract terms may bar it even if OFAC permits reliance.

**Applicability to Unbound.** Multi-sponsor platform: many recipient charities are common across sponsors, so reuse for recipient organizations (public data) has high value. Donor-side dispositions are sponsor-confidential.

**Business assumptions.**

- Recipient entity data is largely public (IRS BMF); donor data is sponsor-confidential.

**Recommended answer.** No by default for donors and advisors. For recipient organizations, allow reuse of a disposition made by a platform-level qualified reviewer, or by another sponsor's officer, only if the relying sponsor has adopted a written reliance policy. Each reuse is recorded as a reliance event for that sponsor, invalidation propagates to every relying sponsor, and the relying sponsor's officer can review the evidence on demand. Liability and reporting stay with each sponsor.

**Remaining uncertainty.** Privacy/contract permissibility of sharing; no OFAC guidance on shared dispositions.

**Consequences if wrong.** Over-reliance on a wrong disposition spreads one error to every relying sponsor; each sponsor is separately liable.

**Conservative fallback.** Sponsor-scoped dispositions only.

**Alternative business or architecture design.** Alternative: platform-operated screening service with documented methodology adopted by each sponsor's board. Cost: platform reviewer staff. Exposure: concentrated but well-controlled. Lost opportunity: none.

**External authorization required.** Each relying sponsor's written adoption of the reliance policy.

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given sponsor B without an adopted reliance policy, when screening hits a recipient dispositioned by sponsor A, then B gets a fresh alert.
- Given A's disposition is invalidated, when invalidation saves, then all reliance events by other sponsors are invalidated and alerts re-open within 24 hours.

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- Sponsor contract terms on data sharing
- OFAC guidance on third-party screening

**Related questions.**

- none

**Corrections to prior records.**

- none

### SPD-CXP-002 — May donor identity proofing be risk-based, with a document and selfie step only when database verification fails or risk is elevated?

**Disposition:** business-policy-decision · **Confidence:** medium · **Jurisdiction:** US-federal guidance (NIST, voluntary for private entities); ACH network rules · **Batch:** F (Privacy, communications and sanctions)

**Original provisional answer.** Recommendation: Yes for release 1 individuals funding by ACH, wire or check from US accounts. Use the document step for entities, complex assets and failed matches.

**Relevant requirements.** SPD-CXP-002, RCD-023

**Authoritative findings.**

- *established* — SP 800-63A-4 is final (July 2025) and defines IAL1-IAL3; IAL1 and IAL2 may be performed remotely. [EV-F-030, Sec. 1; CSRC page]
- *established* — IAL2 permits non-biometric verification: confirmation code sent to a validated address associated with the evidence, or proofing-agent visual comparison (in person, live video or asynchronous). [EV-F-030, Sec. 4.2 (approved non-biometric methods)]
- *established* — Knowledge-based verification shall not be used for identity verification. [EV-F-030, Sec. 2.5 (KBV)]
- *established* — Revision 4 supersedes SP 800-63-3/63A (2020). [EV-F-032, CSRC metadata]

**Supporting interpretation.** DAF sponsors are not, to our knowledge, Bank Secrecy Act 'financial institutions' with a CIP mandate (interpretation, not verified here). Identity proofing is therefore a risk-based control choice, and NIST 800-63A-4 supports risk-based proofing with non-biometric alternatives.

**Competing interpretation.** Banks and custodians the sponsor uses may impose their own KYC on account holders; a sponsor's bank agreement may require it.

**Applicability to Unbound.** Release-1 individuals funding from US bank accounts already pass bank KYC at the funding source. Matching bank-account ownership plus database validation gives evidence comparable to IAL1-plus, with step-up to IAL2-style verification on risk signals.

**Business assumptions.**

- Funding from US bank accounts with ownership verification.
- No BSA CIP obligation applies to the sponsor.

**Recommended answer.** Yes. Use risk-based proofing. Default: validate core attributes against authoritative or credible sources, confirm the funding account owner matches, and send a confirmation code to a validated address/phone. Step up to document plus selfie, or a non-biometric alternative such as a live-video proofing agent, when validation fails or risk is elevated (large contribution, complex assets, foreign address, sanctions near-match). Entities: verify registration and the authority of the signatory, not a selfie. Do not use KBV for verification.

**Remaining uncertainty.** Bank-partner contractual KYC requirements; whether any state imposes donor-verification rules.

**Consequences if wrong.** Weak proofing allows account takeover/fraud, money laundering through DAFs and sanctions evasion.

**Conservative fallback.** Document plus selfie (with non-biometric alternative) for all new donors.

**Alternative business or architecture design.** Alternative: rely on funding-bank KYC plus account ownership verification only. Cost: lowest. Friction: lowest. Exposure: higher for ACH fraud and synthetic identities. Recommended design combines it with step-up.

**External authorization required.** Sponsor bank/treasury confirmation that its bank agreement imposes no stricter KYC.

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given database validation succeeds and the funding account owner matches, when onboarding completes, then no document step is shown.
- Given validation fails, when the donor continues, then document+selfie and a non-biometric agent alternative are both offered.
- Given any flow, then no knowledge-based questions are used to verify identity.

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- Bank partner KYC requirements
- FinCEN rule extending AML duties to DAF sponsors

**Related questions.**

- none

**Corrections to prior records.**

- none

### SPD-GRT-003 — What maximum age and invalidation triggers apply to reuse of human false-positive sanctions dispositions?

**Disposition:** business-policy-decision · **Confidence:** medium · **Jurisdiction:** US-federal (OFAC) · **Batch:** F (Privacy, communications and sanctions)

**Original provisional answer.** Recommendation: Reuse until subject identity attributes or the list entry change, with a counsel-set maximum age (e.g. 12 months [A]).

**Relevant requirements.** SPD-GRT-003, RR-UNB-EXT-004-r1, UNB-RCN-GRT-007, requirements/discovery/2026-11-24-simplification-economics/20-OPEN-DECISIONS-AND-EVIDENCE-GAPS.md

**Authoritative findings.**

- *established* — OFAC does not confirm false positives; organizations document their match analysis and keep records. [EV-F-008, FAQ 5 step 6]
- *established* — Screening must incorporate list updates; failures to update are a recurring root cause of violations. [EV-F-006, Framework p.10 VI]
- *established* — Records kept 10 years after the transaction. [EV-F-001, 501.601]

**Supporting interpretation.** No OFAC rule sets an age for reusing a false-positive disposition. A disposition is valid only for the subject attributes and list-entry content it compared. Event-based invalidation (subject identity attributes change, matched list entry is amended or a new similar entry appears) is the substantive control. A maximum age is a policy backstop.

**Competing interpretation.** Some institutions require periodic re-review of all suppressions (e.g. annually) regardless of events.

**Applicability to Unbound.** Reuse reduces analyst work across repeat grants to the same charity or donor.

**Business assumptions.**

- Screening vendor exposes list-entry versioning or hash.

**Recommended answer.** Reuse a human false-positive disposition until any of these happens: (a) a subject identity attribute changes (name, DOB, address, country, EIN/registration number); (b) the matched list entry is amended, removed or re-added (entry version/hash change); (c) a new list entry produces a match above threshold; (d) 12 months elapse (policy backstop); (e) the dispositioning officer's authority is revoked for cause.
