Every reuse event is logged against the original disposition and retained 10 years.

**Remaining uncertainty.** Max age is judgment; no regulator benchmark located.

**Consequences if wrong.** Stale suppression could let a true match through after a list change (strict liability).

**Conservative fallback.** 6-month maximum age plus event invalidation.

**Alternative business or architecture design.** Alternative: no reuse (re-review every alert). Cost: high analyst time. Exposure: marginally lower. Lost opportunity: automation savings.

**External authorization required.** Sponsor compliance approval of the max-age policy.

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given a stored false-positive for subject S vs entry E version v1, when E becomes v2, then the next screening opens a new alert.
- Given a disposition older than 12 months, when the subject is rescreened, then the alert is re-opened for human review.

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- OFAC guidance on suppression/whitelisting
- Enforcement action citing stale whitelists

**Related questions.**

- none

**Corrections to prior records.**

- none

### UNB-XIN-IAM-003#Q1 — AAL3 for sponsor workforce: hardware key availability.

**Disposition:** established-by-authoritative-evidence · **Confidence:** high · **Jurisdiction:** US-federal guidance (NIST) · **Batch:** F (Privacy, communications and sanctions)

**Original provisional answer.** Product direction approved: require phishing-resistant workforce authentication; reserve AAL3 for justified high-risk roles with compliant authenticators; Security validates NIST mapping. Requirement: Unbound MUST maintain one StepUpPolicy matrix mapping action classes (authenticator/recovery change, authority or delegation change, payee or destination change, data export, grant recommendation above a sponsor threshold, API client/credential creation, sponsor policy publication) to required assurance (AAL2 with phishing-resistant authenticator, or AAL3 for platform and sponsor workforce privile

**Relevant requirements.** UNB-XIN-IAM-003

**Authoritative findings.**

- *established* — AAL3 requires a cryptographic authenticator with a non-exportable private key providing phishing resistance, in a hardware-protected environment; syncable authenticators shall not be used at AAL3. [EV-F-031, SP 800-63B-4 Sec. 2.3.2]
- *established* — At AAL2, verifiers shall offer at least one phishing-resistant option; exportable (syncable) keys are usable at AAL2 or below. [EV-F-031, Sec. 2.2.2; 3.1.6.1]

**Supporting interpretation.** FIDO2 hardware security keys (and device-bound platform authenticators with hardware-protected non-exportable keys) meet AAL3 authenticator properties. They are commercially available at modest per-user cost, so availability is not a blocker (interpretation).

**Competing interpretation.** Some sponsor staff use personal or shared devices, so device-bound platform authenticators may be impractical. Issuing two hardware keys per privileged user (primary plus backup) solves this.

**Applicability to Unbound.** Platform and sponsor privileged roles (payment release, payee change, policy publication, credential admin) are the AAL3 population.

**Business assumptions.**

- Privileged population is small (tens of users per sponsor).

**Recommended answer.** Require AAL3-grade authenticators (hardware security keys or device-bound non-exportable platform keys; no synced passkeys) for platform and sponsor privileged roles. Issue two keys per user and run recovery through an identity-proofed process. Require AAL2 with a phishing-resistant authenticator (synced passkeys allowed) for other workforce. Hardware-key availability is a procurement item, not a feasibility barrier.

**Remaining uncertainty.** Sponsor willingness to procure keys; accessibility needs for some staff.

**Consequences if wrong.** Privileged-account phishing could allow fraudulent payment release.

**Conservative fallback.** Phishing-resistant AAL2 for all workforce if AAL3 keys unavailable, plus dual control on payment release.

**Alternative business or architecture design.** Alternative: AAL2 phishing-resistant for everyone plus dual control. Cost: lower. Exposure: modestly higher.

**External authorization required.** none

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given a user with a privileged role, when authenticating with a synced passkey, then privileged actions are denied with step-up to an AAL3 authenticator.
- Given a privileged user with one registered key, when the role is assigned, then assignment is blocked until a second key is registered.

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- NIST revision
- Sponsor accessibility exception requests

**Related questions.**

- none

**Corrections to prior records.**

- none

### UNB-XIN-NPG-009#Q1 — Is a sponsor (nonprofit corporation) a 'domestic concern' in counsel's view, and what diligence standard applies?

**Disposition:** established-by-authoritative-evidence · **Confidence:** high · **Jurisdiction:** US-federal (FCPA) · **Batch:** F (Privacy, communications and sanctions)

**Original provisional answer.** Product direction approved: record jurisdiction and applicable anti-bribery controls; do not assume exemption for nonprofit; validation gate Counsel. Requirement: Every direct foreign grant MUST, before approval, hold a ForeignNexusDeclaration from the grantee covering: whether the grantee is a government body or instrumentality; whether any officer, director or key employee is a foreign government official or close relative; and whether grant funds will pass to any government official. Any affirmative answer or missing declaration MUST route to CounselRevi

**Relevant requirements.** UNB-XIN-NPG-009, requirements/discovery/2026-11-23-cross-industry/data/gap-requirements.jsonl, requirements/discovery/2026-11-24-final-55-question-resolutions.md

**Authoritative findings.**

- *established* — 'Domestic concern' includes any corporation, association or unincorporated organization organized under US state law or with its US principal place of business; no nonprofit exception. [EV-F-027, 15 U.S.C. 78dd-2(h)(1)(B)]
- *established* — DOJ treated a US-organized nonprofit grantmaker as a domestic concern subject to the anti-bribery provisions. [EV-F-028, Op. Rel. 10-02, p.5 Analysis]
- *established* — DOJ declined enforcement for a foreign grant supported by three-stage due diligence on recipients, staged disbursement, independent monitoring and audits for five years and recipient anti-corruption certifications. [EV-F-028, Op. Rel. 10-02, pp.2-5]
- *interpretation* — DOJ's June 2025 guidelines changed enforcement priorities, not the statute. [EV-F-029, secondary summaries (not fetched)]

**Supporting interpretation.** A sponsor that is a nonprofit corporation organized in a US state is a domestic concern. Liability still requires a corrupt payment to a foreign official to obtain or retain business. Ordinary DAF grants rarely have a business purpose, but Op. Rel. 10-02 shows DOJ treated 'nonprofit business' (a license to operate) as business. The diligence standard is risk-based; no statutory standard exists.

**Competing interpretation.** Some argue charitable grants without a business nexus fall outside the anti-bribery prohibition entirely. That is true as to the business-purpose element, but it does not remove domestic-concern status.

**Applicability to Unbound.** Applies to sponsors (and to Unbound itself, a US company, though Unbound makes no grants). The ForeignNexusDeclaration requirement is a proportionate control for direct foreign grants.

**Business assumptions.**

- Direct foreign grants may be out of R1 scope (per XIF-ADV-003).

**Recommended answer.** Yes. A nonprofit-corporation sponsor is a 'domestic concern' (15 U.S.C. 78dd-2(h)(1)(B); DOJ Op. Rel. 10-02). Use a risk-based diligence standard modelled on Op. Rel. 10-02: a grantee declaration on government ownership and official connections, sanctions/PEP screening, staged payments and grant-use reporting for larger or higher-risk grants, and counsel review on any affirmative answer. Keep the existing requirement as written.

**Remaining uncertainty.** Opinion releases bind only the requestor; DOJ enforcement priorities may change.

**Consequences if wrong.** Low-probability but severe criminal/civil exposure and reputational harm for the sponsor if a grant funds a bribe.

**Conservative fallback.** Route all direct foreign grants through equivalency/expenditure-responsibility review with grantee declarations.

**Alternative business or architecture design.** Avoidance: in R1, allow foreign giving only through US intermediaries (US 501(c)(3) 'friends of' organizations or international intermediaries) that conduct their own FCPA diligence. Cost: intermediary fees of a few percent. Friction: moderate for donors. Exposure: low. Lost opportunity: direct foreign grants deferred.

**External authorization required.** none

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given a direct foreign grant, when submitted for approval without a ForeignNexusDeclaration, then approval is blocked.
- Given a declaration with any affirmative answer, when submitted, then the grant routes to CounselReview and cannot be approved by a standard approver.

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- DOJ FCPA policy changes
- Expansion of direct foreign grants into R1

**Related questions.**

- none

**Corrections to prior records.**

- none

### UNB-XIN-NPG-010#Q1 — Which programs' GLs does counsel accept for DAF grants?

**Disposition:** reasoned-interpretation · **Confidence:** medium · **Jurisdiction:** US-federal (OFAC) · **Batch:** F (Privacy, communications and sanctions)

**Original provisional answer.** Product direction approved: version approved license scope, conditions and expiry; block unsupported reliance; validation gate Sanctions counsel. Requirement: Any grant whose recipient, destination country or payment chain falls within a sanctions program MUST be held unless a SanctionsAuthorization record exists citing a specific license number or a general license (program, section, version) with counsel's applicability determination and its conditions (e.g. recordkeeping, excluded parties). Release MUST verify the authorization is unexpired and its c

**Relevant requirements.** UNB-XIN-NPG-010, UNB-RCN-REG-003

**Authoritative findings.**

- *established* — December 2022 actions issued or amended GLs across many programs for NGO humanitarian and other activities; existing program-specific NGO GLs (e.g. Iran) were not amended; conditions differ by program. [EV-F-010, FAQ 1105, 1108]
- *established* — Example NGO GL: authorizes transactions ordinarily incident to listed non-commercial civilian-benefit activities by a non-blocked NGO; excludes funds transfers with knowledge or reason to know the beneficiary is blocked (except taxes, fees, permits, utilities). [EV-F-011, 31 CFR 594.520(a)-(c)]
- *established* — NGO GLs are codified program by program under different section numbers. [EV-F-012, eCFR search results]
- *established* — Records of transactions under a license must be kept 10 years. [EV-F-001, 501.601 ('regardless of whether such transaction is effected pursuant to license')]

**Supporting interpretation.** A sponsor funding a foreign NGO's covered activity is engaging in a transaction 'ordinarily incident and necessary' to that NGO's activities, so the NGO GLs plausibly cover the grant if the grantee is a non-blocked NGO, the activity fits the listed categories and no blocked beneficiary is known (interpretation). Reliance must be pinned to program, CFR section, FR citation and eCFR date, or to GL number and letter suffix for web-issued GLs.

**Competing interpretation.** Some programs (comprehensive embargoes such as Cuba, Iran, North Korea) have narrower NGO GLs, registration requirements (e.g. 538.521 Sudan-era registration, a legacy section) or specific-license needs. Applicability per program needs program-specific reading.

**Applicability to Unbound.** DAF sponsors rarely grant into sanctioned jurisdictions directly. The safe architecture holds such grants and requires a counsel-approved SanctionsAuthorization record.

**Business assumptions.**

- Grants into sanctioned jurisdictions are rare and can be deferred.

**Recommended answer.** Accept by default only the targeted-program NGO GLs of the December 2022 type (e.g. 31 CFR 594.520 and analogous sections) for grants to non-blocked NGOs for listed activities, after automated checks (grantee not blocked, activity category recorded, no known blocked beneficiary). Comprehensive-embargo programs (Cuba, Iran, North Korea, covered regions of Ukraine/Russia) are held for program-specific approval or a specific license. Version each reliance as {program, CFR section or GL number+suffix, FR citation, eCFR as-of date, conditions}. Re-verify at payment release that the eCFR version is unchanged.

**Remaining uncertainty.** Program-by-program differences not exhaustively reviewed; OFAC 2023 NGO fact sheet not fetched.

**Consequences if wrong.** A grant made under a misapplied GL is an unlicensed prohibited transaction (strict liability).

**Conservative fallback.** Hold all grants touching any sanctioned jurisdiction and require OFAC specific license or counsel memo.

**Alternative business or architecture design.** Avoidance: R1 excludes grants to recipients or destinations in any OFAC country-based program; donors may use US intermediaries holding their own licenses. Cost: lost niche giving. Friction: low. Exposure: minimal.

**External authorization required.** OFAC specific license for any activity not within a general license; sponsor approval of the GL catalog.

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given a grant whose destination is in a sanctions program, when no SanctionsAuthorization record exists, then the grant is held.
- Given a SanctionsAuthorization citing a CFR section, when the eCFR version date for that section changes before release, then release is blocked pending re-approval.

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- OFAC amends or revokes a program (e.g. Syria program changes in 2025)
- New humanitarian GLs

**Related questions.**

- none

**Corrections to prior records.**

- none

### UNB-XIN-SVC-003#Q1 — Is continuation after disclosure acceptable consent in all target states (counsel)?

**Disposition:** reasoned-interpretation · **Confidence:** medium · **Jurisdiction:** US states (all-party consent laws); US-federal · **Batch:** F (Privacy, communications and sanctions)

**Original provisional answer.** Product direction approved (2026-11-24 review): do not assume continued participation constitutes consent; support explicit consent and no-recording alternative.

**Relevant requirements.** UNB-XIN-SVC-003, XID-SVC-002

**Authoritative findings.**

- *established* — Advising all parties at the outset of intent to record defeats a California 632 claim; a party may decline to continue. [EV-F-033, Kearney opinion text]
- *established* — All-party states listed by RCFP; assume the stricter law for interstate calls. [EV-F-036, RCFP Introduction]
- *interpretation* — 632.7 requires consent of all parties for covered cellular calls. [EV-F-034, Smith v. LoanMe summaries]

**Supporting interpretation.** Consent is per party; all-party statutes are satisfied only when every party (including late joiners) knows of the recording and continues or agrees.

**Competing interpretation.** Some courts infer consent from prior notice in the same call session for transferred parties who are employees of the recording business (they consented via employment policy).

**Applicability to Unbound.** Relevant only if recording is enabled after R1.

**Business assumptions.**

- Recording disabled at R1.

**Recommended answer.** Do not rely on continuation as consent in all target states. California: an outset advisement suffices (Kearney). Elsewhere it is not uniformly established, so the uniform design requires explicit affirmative consent (DTMF or verbal) and offers an unrecorded path. R1 records nothing.

**Remaining uncertainty.** State-by-state case law on implied consent not reviewed.

**Consequences if wrong.** CIPA and similar statutory damages and class exposure.

**Conservative fallback.** Explicit consent from every party; pause recording on joins.

**Alternative business or architecture design.** Avoidance: no recording (R1). Cost: lost QA evidence. Exposure: none.

**External authorization required.** none

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given recording enabled, when a caller hears the disclosure but gives no affirmative response, then recording does not start.

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- Change in state law
- Decision to enable recording

**Related questions.**

- none

**Corrections to prior records.**

- none

### UNB-XIN-SVC-003#Q2 — Does a transfer to a third party require re-disclosure?

**Disposition:** reasoned-interpretation · **Confidence:** medium · **Jurisdiction:** US states (all-party consent laws); US-federal · **Batch:** F (Privacy, communications and sanctions)

**Original provisional answer.** Product direction approved (2026-11-24 review): re-disclose recording and data sharing when required; preserve consent scope and transfer evidence.

**Relevant requirements.** UNB-XIN-SVC-003, XID-SVC-002

**Authoritative findings.**

- *established* — Advising all parties at the outset of intent to record defeats a California 632 claim; a party may decline to continue. [EV-F-033, Kearney opinion text]
- *established* — All-party states listed by RCFP; assume the stricter law for interstate calls. [EV-F-036, RCFP Introduction]
- *interpretation* — 632.7 requires consent of all parties for covered cellular calls. [EV-F-034, Smith v. LoanMe summaries]

**Supporting interpretation.** Consent is per party; all-party statutes are satisfied only when every party (including late joiners) knows of the recording and continues or agrees.

**Competing interpretation.** Some courts infer consent from prior notice in the same call session for transferred parties who are employees of the recording business (they consented via employment policy).

**Applicability to Unbound.** Relevant only if recording is enabled after R1.

**Business assumptions.**

- Recording disabled at R1.

**Recommended answer.** Yes. Any party newly joining the call (transfer or conference to a sponsor officer, charity or vendor) must receive the recording disclosure and give consent before recording continues with them; otherwise pause recording for that segment. Record transfer events with consent status. Disclose onward data sharing of the recording separately, under the privacy notice.

**Remaining uncertainty.** State-by-state case law on implied consent not reviewed.

**Consequences if wrong.** CIPA and similar statutory damages and class exposure.

**Conservative fallback.** Explicit consent from every party; pause recording on joins.

**Alternative business or architecture design.** Avoidance: no recording (R1). Cost: lost QA evidence. Exposure: none.

**External authorization required.** none

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given recording active, when a new party joins, then recording pauses until that party consents.

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- Change in state law
- Decision to enable recording

**Related questions.**

- none

**Corrections to prior records.**

- none

### UNB-XIN-SVC-004#Q1 — Assurance levels naming (NIST AAL mapping?).

**Disposition:** reasoned-interpretation · **Confidence:** high · **Jurisdiction:** US-federal guidance (NIST) · **Batch:** F (Privacy, communications and sanctions)

**Original provisional answer.** Product direction approved: separate contact assurance from authentication AAL; map only verified equivalences. Requirement: Unbound MUST maintain a versioned ContactAssuranceMatrix mapping (channel x achieved assurance level x principal role) to permitted disclosure classes (public, account-summary, transaction-detail, personal-data) and permitted action classes (none, draft-only, change-pending-confirmation). Knowledge-based answers alone MUST NOT achieve more than the lowest assurance level and MUST NOT permit disclo

**Relevant requirements.** UNB-XIN-SVC-004

**Authoritative findings.**

- *established* — AALs describe confidence that a claimant controls authenticators bound to a subscriber account (AAL1 basic, AAL2 high, AAL3 very high). [EV-F-031, Sec. 2.1-2.3]
- *established* — KBV shall not be used for identity verification. [EV-F-030, Sec. 2.5]

**Supporting interpretation.** Contact channels (phone, chat, email) often verify callers by callback, OTP or knowledge questions. Only verification through a bound authenticator (in-app push or passkey challenge during the contact) corresponds to an AAL. Other methods need their own scale.

**Competing interpretation.** Using AAL names for contact assurance is attractive but misleading, because AAL requires authenticator properties a phone agent cannot verify.

**Applicability to Unbound.** Name the levels separately (e.g. CAL0-CAL3) and record an explicit mapping only where the contact was verified by an authenticator challenge (CAL = achieved AAL).

**Business assumptions.**

- none recorded

**Recommended answer.** Use a distinct 'Contact Assurance Level' (CAL0 unverified; CAL1 knowledge or caller-ID only, public info only; CAL2 OTP to a validated phone/email, or callback to the number on record; CAL3 in-session authenticator challenge at AAL2+). Map CAL3 to the achieved AAL; no other equivalence. KBV can never exceed CAL1, consistent with NIST's KBV prohibition.

**Remaining uncertainty.** Calibration of CAL2 permissions is a policy choice.

**Consequences if wrong.** Confusing names could lead to over-disclosure on weak verification.

**Conservative fallback.** Treat every non-authenticator contact as CAL1.

**Alternative business or architecture design.** Alternative: route all sensitive requests to the authenticated app (no phone disclosure). Cost: donor friction. Exposure: lowest.

**External authorization required.** none

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given a caller verified only by knowledge answers, when requesting transaction detail, then disclosure is denied.
- Given a caller who approves an in-app push during the call, when recorded, then CAL3 with the AAL of the authenticator is stored.

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- NIST guidance on contact-center verification

**Related questions.**

- none

**Corrections to prior records.**

- none

### XID-CRM-001 — Confirm provisional decision: Is sponsor email promoting DAF services 'commercial' under CAN-SPAM; which entity is sender in white-label?

**Disposition:** reasoned-interpretation · **Confidence:** high · **Jurisdiction:** US-federal (CAN-SPAM; FTC), states (CAN-SPAM enforcement and preemption carve-outs) · **Batch:** F (Privacy, communications and sanctions)

**Original provisional answer.** Promotional email about DAF services is treated as commercial under CAN-SPAM. In white-label programs the sender is the entity named in the From line, designated contractually as the single sender; suppression is keyed by that sender. Transactional and legally required messages are classified separately.

**Relevant requirements.** UNB-XIN-CRM-003, XID-INT-001, UNB-XIN-REG-003

**Authoritative findings.**

- *established* — CAN-SPAM does not set up a dichotomy between commercial and nonprofit messages; when nonprofits send emails whose primary purpose is promoting a commercial product or service, recipients are protected. [EV-F-017, 73 FR 29655 Part II.A.1]
- *established* — FTC lacks CAN-SPAM jurisdiction over entities not organized for profit, but states and internet access providers can bring actions against nonprofits. [EV-F-017, 73 FR 29655 n.17]
- *established* — Primary purpose tests: exclusively promotional = commercial; mixed with transactional content = commercial if subject line suggests promotion or transactional content is not at the beginning; mixed with other content = commercial if subject line or body reading indicates promotional primary purpose. [EV-F-016, 16 CFR 316.3(a)(1)-(3)]
- *established* — Transactional or relationship content includes account statements, changes in terms or status for an ongoing account relationship, and delivery of goods/services the recipient is entitled to. [EV-F-016, 16 CFR 316.3(c)(3), (5)]
- *established* — Single designated sender is available only when more than one person's products are advertised, and only if that person is a statutory sender, is identified in the From line as sole sender and complies with the listed duties. [EV-F-016, 16 CFR 316.2(m)]
- *established* — Both the company whose product is promoted and the company that actually sends may be held responsible; opt-outs honored within 10 business days. [EV-F-018, FTC guide, 'Monitor what others are doing on your behalf']

**Supporting interpretation.** Whether a DAF account is a 'commercial product or service' is not settled. It is a charitable giving service, but sponsors charge fees and compete with other sponsors. Treating promotional DAF emails as commercial is therefore the prudent reading (interpretation). Treating them as commercial costs little (an unsubscribe link, a postal address, no deceptive headers), and Unbound is a for-profit company under FTC jurisdiction that may be the 'initiator' of these emails.

**Competing interpretation.** Pure donation appeals and mission content from a 501(c)(3) are likely not commercial speech (316.3 n.1), so a sponsor could argue such emails are outside CAN-SPAM. Unbound might also argue its role is 'routine conveyance' for sponsor-authored lists (statutory term, not fetched).

**Applicability to Unbound.** In white-label deployments the sponsor's DAF program is the product promoted, so the sponsor is the 'sender'. The From line alone does not make someone the sender. Unbound, as a for-profit transmitter, can be liable as initiator. The 316.2(m) designated-sender rule matters only when an email promotes more than one person's products (for example a sponsor email that also promotes an advisor firm or Unbound).

**Business assumptions.**

- Sponsor marketing emails promote the sponsor's DAF program and may co-promote partner advisors.

**Recommended answer.** Confirm with corrections. Classify each template as commercial, transactional/relationship or other (316.3) before activation. Promotional DAF emails get commercial controls. Statements, grant confirmations and term-change notices are transactional (316.3(c)) but still carry accurate headers. The sender is the person whose product or service is promoted, and in white-label programs that is the sponsor. Where a message promotes more than one party, designate one sender only when that party meets all of 316.2(m)(A)-(C). Key suppression to the sender and honor opt-outs within 10 business days across all platforms that send for that sender.

**Remaining uncertainty.** Whether DAF account promotion is 'commercial product or service' remains untested; state anti-spam laws not fully catalogued (CAN-SPAM largely preempts them except for falsity/deception).

**Consequences if wrong.** If promotional emails without commercial controls are found commercial: FTC civil penalties against for-profit initiators (Unbound) per email and state/ISP actions against sponsors.

**Conservative fallback.** Apply commercial controls to every non-transactional email, including donation appeals.

**Alternative business or architecture design.** Alternative: apply commercial controls (unsubscribe, address, honest headers) to all non-transactional email regardless of classification. Cost: negligible. Friction: none. Exposure: minimal. Lost opportunity: none. Recommended as the default.

**External authorization required.** none

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given a template marked promotional, when activation is attempted without unsubscribe link and valid postal address, then activation is blocked.
- Given a recipient opts out from sponsor S, when any message classed commercial for S is queued after 10 business days, then it is suppressed.
- Given a message promoting S and advisor firm A, when sent, then either both are treated as senders with both suppression lists applied or S is designated sole sender with 316.2(m)(A)-(C) checks recorded.
- Given a transactional statement email, when its body begins with promotional content, then the classifier reclassifies it as commercial (316.3(a)(2)(ii)).

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- FTC amends 16 CFR 316
- Litigation on DAF marketing emails
- Unbound brand appears in sponsor emails

**Related questions.**

- **UNB-XIN-CRM-003#Q1**: Treat sponsor email promoting DAF services as commercial. The FTC treats nonprofit messages whose primary purpose promotes a commercial product or service as covered (73 FR 29655). Whether a DAF is such a product is not decided, so the conservative classification is commercial. Transactional/relationship messages are defined in 16 CFR 316.3(c).
- **XID-INT-001**: Yes, P0 for all channels including email. Email needs CAN-SPAM classification (16 CFR 316.3). SMS and prerecorded voice need TCPA consent classification (47 CFR 64.1200). Activation of any outbound template should require a recorded classification.

**Corrections to prior records.**

- XID-CRM-001 decision 'sender is the entity named in the From line, designated contractually as the single sender': Under CAN-SPAM the sender is the person who initiates the message and whose product is promoted. 16 CFR 316.2(m) designation applies only to multi-advertiser messages and requires From-line identification plus compliance; a contract or the From line alone does not make someone the sender. → Sender = the person whose product or service is promoted (the sponsor in white-label). Use the 316.2(m) designation only for multi-advertiser messages that meet (A)-(C). Unbound remains potentially liable as initiator.
- XID-CRM-001 rationale 'CAN-SPAM applies to any person sending commercial messages; nonprofit status is not an exemption': This is substantially correct but leaves out that the FTC lacks jurisdiction over nonprofits, so states and ISPs are the enforcers against them, and that Unbound (for-profit) is within FTC reach. → Add 73 FR 29655 and n.17 as authority.

### XID-REG-003 — Confirm provisional decision: Does the for-profit operator sending SMS on a sponsor's behalf retain any nonprofit TCPA exemption?

**Disposition:** reasoned-interpretation · **Confidence:** high · **Jurisdiction:** US-federal (TCPA/FCC); federal circuits split on texts under 227(c) · **Batch:** F (Privacy, communications and sanctions)

**Original provisional answer.** Automated or prerecorded calls and texts sent by the operator on behalf of the sponsor (a tax-exempt nonprofit) require at least prior express consent, which the regulation permits for calls 'made by or on behalf of a tax-exempt nonprofit organization'. Messages sent on behalf of a for-profit partner, or that advertise a for-profit's services, require prior express written consent.

**Relevant requirements.** UNB-XIN-REG-003, UNB-XIN-CRM-003

**Authoritative findings.**

- *established* — The regulation extends the nonprofit standard to calls 'made by or on behalf of a tax-exempt nonprofit organization': advertising/telemarketing robocalls to wireless numbers need only prior express consent (not written). [EV-F-019, 47 CFR 64.1200(a)(2)]
- *established* — Residential prerecorded calls by or on behalf of a nonprofit are exempt if no more than three per 30 days and opt-outs are honored; nonprofit calls are excluded from 'telephone solicitation'. [EV-F-019, 64.1200(a)(3)(iv); (f)(15)(iii)]
- *established* — The statute's 'telephone solicitation' exclusion covers calls 'by a tax exempt nonprofit organization'; the 'on behalf of' extension is in the FCC rule, not the statutory text. [EV-F-020, 47 U.S.C. 227(a)(4)(C)]
- *established* — ATDS or prerecorded/artificial-voice calls to wireless numbers require prior express consent with no nonprofit exception. [EV-F-020, 227(b)(1)(A)(iii)]
- *interpretation* — Text messages qualify as calls under 227(b)(1)(A)(iii) (stated as undisputed by the Supreme Court). [EV-F-025, 577 U.S. at 156 (not fetched)]
- *established* — Seventh Circuit (July 2026) held marketing texts are not 'telephone calls' under 227(c)(5) (DNC private claims), applying McLaughlin (2025); 227(b) text claims unaffected. [EV-F-026, H&K summary of Steidinger]
- *established* — One-to-one consent and 'logically and topically related' requirements vacated (11th Cir. Jan 24, 2025); FCC removed the text effective with the April 30, 2025 mandate. [EV-F-023, 90 FR 42137 abstract]
- *established* — From Nov 25, 2026, revocation of informational robocalls/texts may be treated as category-specific; revocation of advertising/telemarketing covers all such messages from that caller; callers may designate exclusive revocation means; honor within 10 business days. [EV-F-021, 91 FR 67214; amended 64.1200(a)(10)-(12)]
- *established* — FCC is considering shorter revocation timing, mandatory revoke-all and affiliate treatment (proposed only). [EV-F-022, FNPRM abstract]

**Supporting interpretation.** The regulation's 'on behalf of' language covers an agent or vendor (including a for-profit operator) sending for the tax-exempt sponsor's own program. So for the sponsor's messages, prior express consent suffices even where content is promotional. A for-profit partner's own marketing does not qualify.

**Competing interpretation.** After McLaughlin Chiropractic v. McKesson (2025), district courts are not bound by FCC interpretations, and the statute's 'telephone solicitation' exclusion says only 'by a tax exempt nonprofit'. A court could therefore deny the exemption to a for-profit vendor's calls. Separately, the 'nonprofit' exemptions do not relieve the wireless-number consent requirement for ATDS/prerecorded messages.

**Applicability to Unbound.** Unbound sends SMS and possibly prerecorded voice for sponsors. Most texts go to wireless numbers, where prior express consent is needed regardless. The practical gap between prior express consent and prior express written consent can be closed cheaply at onboarding.

**Business assumptions.**

- Sponsor is a 501(c)(3) and messages concern its own DAF program.
- Unbound's SMS platform may or may not be an ATDS under Facebook v. Duguid (not analysed here).

**Recommended answer.** Confirm with hardening. (1) Capture consent at onboarding in a form that meets prior express written consent (signed e-consent naming the sponsor, stating it is not a condition of service) for every automated/prerecorded text or call. This makes the 'on behalf of' question irrelevant. (2) Messages advertising a for-profit partner require written consent naming that partner. (3) Residential prerecorded calls: limit to 3 per 30 days, keep a company-specific DNC list and give identification. (4) Implement revocation per the rules effective Nov 25, 2026: either designate an exclusive revocation means and disclose it in each text, or accept any reasonable means. Treat informational revocations per category and advertising revocations as all-advertising. Honor within 10 business days (target: immediate). (5) Track the FNPRM.

**Remaining uncertainty.** Judicial acceptance of 'on behalf of' for for-profit vendors after McLaughlin; ATDS status of Unbound's sending stack; Seventh Circuit vs other circuits on texts under 227(c).

**Consequences if wrong.** TCPA statutory damages of $500-$1,500 per message, with class-action exposure.

**Conservative fallback.** Written-standard consent for all automated messages; no prerecorded voice in R1.

**Alternative business or architecture design.** Avoidance: R1 SMS only for transactional/informational messages the donor triggered (one-time codes, grant status) with written-standard consent captured in the onboarding flow; no marketing SMS until counsel review. Cost: low. Friction: one checkbox. Exposure: minimal. Lost opportunity: SMS marketing channel deferred.

**External authorization required.** none

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given a donor without a recorded written-standard consent for sponsor S, when an automated text for S is queued, then it is blocked.
- Given a text reply 'STOP' to an advertising message, when processed, then all advertising messages from that caller are suppressed within 10 business days (target within 1 minute) and one confirmation text without marketing is sent within 5 minutes.
- Given a reply 'stop' to a grant-status informational message, when processed, then that informational category is suppressed and other categories continue unless the donor asked to stop all.
- Given an exclusive revocation means is designated, when any text is sent, then the text contains the standardized opt-out word disclosure.
- Given a residential line, when a 4th prerecorded nonprofit call within 30 days is queued, then it is blocked.

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- Court decisions on 'on behalf of' post-McLaughlin
- FCC action on the Oct 2026 FNPRM
- Supreme Court resolution of texts-as-calls
- Introduction of marketing SMS

**Related questions.**

- **UNB-XIN-REG-003#Q1**: The FCC rule extends the nonprofit standard to calls made 'on behalf of' a tax-exempt nonprofit (47 CFR 64.1200(a)(2), (a)(3)(iv), (f)(15)(iii)), so a for-profit operator sending for the sponsor's own program does not lose it under the rule. The statute says only 'by' a nonprofit (227(a)(4)(C)), and post-McLaughlin courts may read it independently. Wireless ATDS/prerecorded messages need prior express consent in any case. Capture written-standard consent to remove the dependency.

**Corrections to prior records.**

- XID-REG-003 decision authority '47 CFR 64.1200(a)(2), (a)(3)(iv), (f)(15)(iii)': The citations are accurate. The record omits that the statute says only 'by' a nonprofit, the McLaughlin deference change, and the Nov 25, 2026 revocation amendments. → Add 47 U.S.C. 227(a)(4)(C), 91 FR 67214 (FCC 26-67) and the one-to-one vacatur (90 FR 42137). Recommend written-standard consent capture.

### XID-SVC-002 — Confirm provisional decision: Record calls at release 1? If yes, is a uniform all-party disclosure with continuation-as-consent acceptable in all target states, and may an AI vendor transcribe?

**Disposition:** established-by-authoritative-evidence · **Confidence:** high · **Jurisdiction:** US-federal (ECPA) and state wiretap/eavesdropping laws · **Batch:** F (Privacy, communications and sanctions)

**Original provisional answer.** Decided: No call recording and no machine transcription of voice calls at release 1. If enabled later: a uniform explicit all-party consent prompt at the start of every call, no recording without an affirmative answer, and a transcription vendor contracted as a service provider with no training use.

**Relevant requirements.** UNB-XIN-SVC-003, requirements/discovery/2026-11-23-cross-industry/16-LEGAL-AND-ACCOUNTING-DECISIONS.md

**Authoritative findings.**

- *established* — Federal law permits recording by a party or with one party's consent (absent criminal/tortious purpose). [EV-F-035, 18 U.S.C. 2511(2)(d)]
- *established* — California applies Penal Code 632 to out-of-state businesses recording calls with California residents; advising all parties at the outset of the intent to record defeats a 632 violation, because a party may decline to continue. [EV-F-033, Kearney, 39 Cal.4th 95 (opinion text)]
- *interpretation* — Penal Code 632.7 bars parties and non-parties from recording covered cellular/cordless calls without all parties' consent. [EV-F-034, Smith v. LoanMe (summaries; not fetched)]
- *established* — All-party consent states per RCFP: CA, DE, FL, IL, MD, MA, MI (qualified), MT, NH, PA, WA; CT and NV have phone-specific nuances; for interstate calls assume the stricter law applies. [EV-F-036, RCFP Introduction]

**Supporting interpretation.** No recording at R1 avoids the issue entirely. If recording is enabled later, a uniform disclosure at the start of every call plus explicit affirmative consent satisfies the strictest states. Kearney shows that an outset disclosure alone suffices in California.

**Competing interpretation.** Continuation-after-notice is widely treated as implied consent, but not uniformly across all-party states. AI transcription vendors have faced CIPA s.631 'third-party eavesdropper' claims (not verified here).

**Applicability to Unbound.** Unbound telephony adapters serve donors in all states.

**Business assumptions.**

- Recording is not needed for R1 operations.

**Recommended answer.** Confirm: no call recording or machine transcription at R1. When enabling: (1) disclose at the outset to every party, including parties added later; (2) require affirmative consent (DTMF or verbal yes); (3) provide a no-recording path; (4) contract the transcription vendor as a service provider with no training use and no independent use, to reduce CIPA s.631 third-party exposure. Maintain a jurisdiction table (state, statute, all/one-party, medium nuance, last verified date) for review, but apply the uniform strictest rule.

**Remaining uncertainty.** State list secondary (RCFP); vendor-eavesdropper case law not reviewed.

**Consequences if wrong.** Recording without all-party consent risks CIPA statutory damages ($5,000 per violation under 637.2, from memory) and class actions.

**Conservative fallback.** Recording disabled.

**Alternative business or architecture design.** Avoidance (current decision): recording disabled. Cost: loses QA/training data and dispute evidence. Exposure: none. Alternative: chat/email channels with written transcripts by design (consent through terms) for QA.

**External authorization required.** none

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given R1 configuration, when any call connects, then no audio is recorded or streamed to a transcription service.
- Given recording enabled later, when a party does not affirmatively consent, then recording does not start and the call continues unrecorded.

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- Sponsor requests recording
- State law changes

**Related questions.**

- **UNB-XIN-SVC-003#Q1**: Not established for all target states. Kearney (Cal. 2006) holds that an outset advisement suffices in California, since a party may decline to continue. Other all-party states are not uniformly settled, so require explicit affirmative consent.
- **UNB-XIN-SVC-003#Q2**: Yes for any newly joining party. All-party statutes require the consent of all parties, so a third party added by transfer or conference must hear the disclosure and consent before recording continues with them.

**Corrections to prior records.**

- none

### RCD-005 — Confirm provisional decision: May a sponsor board delegate routine grant approval to a version-pinned deterministic policy, and who is the fund manager for §4966/§4967?

**Disposition:** external-authorization-required · **Confidence:** medium · **Jurisdiction:** US federal (§4966) and US states (sampled) · **Batch:** G (Assurance, internal control and board delegation)

**Original provisional answer.** Register context: Decided: Build the deterministic approval tier. Until counsel and the sponsor board approve delegation, its outcome is 'ApproveRecommended', which a human approves in one-click batches (A4). After approval the same rule outcome becomes the A3 approval itself, with no code change, only an authority-flag change. \| confirmation_answer: Deterministic recommendation until board delegation and counsel identify accountable fund managers.

**Relevant requirements.** RCD-005, requirements/reconciliation/2026-11-23/14-OPEN-DECISIONS.md:34, requirements/reconciliation/2026-11-23/06-REGULATORY-ASSUMPTION-REGISTER.md:56, requirements/grants/END-TO-END-GRANT-LIFECYCLE-AND-OPERATING-MODEL.md:346, SPD-CLR-004

**Authoritative findings.**

- *established* — Corporate powers exercised by or under the direction of the board; board may delegate management of activities to any person, management company or committee if activities remain under the board's ultimate direction. [EV-G-017, §5210]
- *established* — Board committees may exercise board authority except eight listed matters; grant approval and fund acceptance are not listed. [EV-G-018, §5212(a)(1)-(8)]
- *established* — Board committees need 3+ directors; committees of the corporation cannot bind the board. [EV-G-021, §712]
- *established* — Corporation managed by its board except as certificate provides. [EV-G-020, §701(a)]
- *established* — Business and affairs managed by or under the direction of the board; committees per resolution/bylaws; applies to nonstock corporations. [EV-G-016, §141(a),(c)(2),(j)]
- *established* — Powers exercised by or under the authority of the board; officers' duties per bylaws or board resolution; committees may not authorize distributions or fundamental transactions. [EV-G-023, §§8.01(b), 8.25(e), 8.41]
- *established* — Fund manager = officers, directors, trustees (or individuals with similar powers) and, for any act, employees having authority or responsibility for it; 5% tax on fund manager agreement knowing distribution is taxable, capped at $10,000 per distribution, joint and several. [EV-G-025, §4966(a)(2),(b),(d)(3)]
- *established* — 2023 proposed §4966 regulations not finalized as of May 2026. [EV-G-029, status]

**Supporting interpretation.** Nonprofit statutes in the sampled states (CA, NY, DE, Model-Act state VT) let boards act 'by or under the direction of' the board and delegate to officers, committees or agents while retaining oversight; fund acceptance and grant approval are not on any non-delegable list found. A deterministic policy is not a legal person: the lawful structure is a board resolution adopting the policy and delegating its execution to a named officer or committee who remains accountable, with board oversight reporting. 'Committee' authority limits (director-only membership, 2+ in CA, 3+ in NY) apply only when a board committee is used. California's 'distributions' bar for committees (VT §8.25(e)) concerns distributions to members, not charitable grants (interpretation). Fund managers under §4966(d)(3): the sponsor's officers, directors and trustees always; for a specific distribution also employees with authority or responsibility for it. A deterministic rule does not remove fund managers; those who adopted the policy and the officer to whom execution is delegated are the accountable persons. Tax attaches only to agreement 'knowing' the distribution is taxable, so a sound, tested policy reduces exposure. Unbound staff are not sponsor employees; they would be fund managers only if given officer-like powers (see SPD-ORG-002).

**Competing interpretation.** Some counsel may say grant approval is a core board function requiring human review of each grant; statutes sampled do not say so.

**Applicability to Unbound.** Grant approval tier and ExciseTaxIncident party records.

**Business assumptions.**

- none recorded

**Recommended answer.** Confirm the provisional decision. Delegation to a policy executed under a named officer/committee is permissible in sampled states once the board adopts it; fund managers are the sponsor's officers/directors/trustees and responsible employees (incl. the delegate officer). Record them per policy version. Proposed §4966 regs are not final; do not rely on them.

**Remaining uncertainty.** Bylaws; unsampled states; final §4966 regulations.

**Consequences if wrong.** Fund manager excise tax exposure up to $10,000 per distribution; governance finding.

**Conservative fallback.** ApproveRecommended with one-click human batch (A4).

**Alternative business or architecture design.** Batch human approval (minutes/day; lost automation benefit small at low volume).

**External authorization required.** Sponsor board resolution adopting the grant-approval policy and naming the delegate; counsel sign-off on bylaws.

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given an auto-approved grant, when recorded, then the policy version, adopting resolution and delegate officer are stored as accountable fund managers.

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- Final §4966 regulations published.
- State law change.

**Related questions.**

- none

**Corrections to prior records.**

- none

### RCD-031 — Confirm provisional decision: SOC 1 versus SOC 2 scope.

**Disposition:** established-by-authoritative-evidence · **Confidence:** high · **Jurisdiction:** US · **Batch:** G (Assurance, internal control and board delegation)

**Original provisional answer.** Register context: Decided: Design for SOC 1 Type II (fund-accounting controls) and SOC 2 Type II (platform security). Readiness only; no attestation claimed. \| confirmation_answer: SOC1/2 readiness from inception; actual attestation scope and timing based on reliance.

**Relevant requirements.** RCD-031, requirements/governance/SOC2-CONTROLS-EVIDENCE-AND-ENTERPRISE-DIRECTIVE.md:118, requirements/discovery/2026-11-24-simplification-economics/02-REQUIREMENT-SIMPLIFICATION-MATRIX.md, requirements/discovery/2026-11-24-confirmation-question-review.md

**Authoritative findings.**

- *established* — A SOC 1 report addresses controls at a service organization relevant to user entities' ICFR; criteria are in AT-C 320 .15-.17; its intended users are service-org management, user entities and their auditors; a type 2 may provide evidence of operating effectiveness. [EV-G-004, pp.1-3 rows 'criteria' and 'purpose']
- *established* — SOC 2 covers security, availability, processing integrity, confidentiality or privacy using DC 200 and TSP 100 (2017 TSC with revised points of focus 2022). [EV-G-004, p.2 row 'criteria']
- *established* — Type 1 = design as of a date; type 2 = design and operating effectiveness throughout a period, with tests and results. [EV-G-005, SOC 1 section]
- *established* — The 2022 revision changed points of focus and implementation guidance, not the TSC criteria or SOC 2 description criteria. [EV-G-007, p.1]

**Supporting interpretation.** Splitting scope is consistent with the AICPA framework: SOC 1 for fund-ledger, contribution, grant and reconciliation control objectives relevant to sponsors' financial statements; SOC 2 (Security mandatory, plus Availability, Processing Integrity, Confidentiality as committed) for platform trust. 'Readiness only' is correct: no attestation exists until a CPA firm issues a report.

**Competing interpretation.** 

**Applicability to Unbound.** Design control register tags SOC1/SOC2 per control; readiness only.

**Business assumptions.**

- none recorded

**Recommended answer.** Confirm with clarification: design for SOC 1 (AT-C 320; ledger/ICFR objectives) and SOC 2 (AT-C 105/205 with TSP 100 2017 TSC, revised points of focus 2022). Type 2 requires an observation period (commonly 6-12 months) so readiness evidence must exist from the period start. Actual report scope and timing follow auditor and contract reliance (see SPD-PLT-001).

**Remaining uncertainty.** AT-C 205/AT-C 320 primary texts not fetched (gated); relied on AICPA-derived comparison.

**Consequences if wrong.** Mis-scoped controls create gaps in the first report period.

**Conservative fallback.** Tag controls for both reports where in doubt.

**Alternative business or architecture design.** SOC 2 with Processing Integrity only (no SOC 1) is cheaper but user auditors generally need SOC 1 for ICFR reliance; keep both in readiness.

**External authorization required.** none

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given the control register, when viewed, then each control lists the report(s) it supports and no marketing text claims attestation.

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- AICPA issues new TSC or SOC 1 guide revisions.

**Related questions.**

- **SPD-PLT-001**: Timing by reliance and contract.

**Corrections to prior records.**

- RCD-031 decision text: Does not cite the governing standards or the observation-period implication of Type II. → Name AT-C 320 (SOC 1) and AT-C 205 + TSP 100 2017 TSC (PoF 2022) for SOC 2; note Type 2 needs evidence from the start of the examination period.

### RR-ADM-003-r1#Q1 — Does the sponsor's state nonprofit law permit board delegation of fund acceptance?

**Disposition:** external-authorization-required · **Confidence:** medium · **Jurisdiction:** US states (sampled: CA, NY, DE, VT/Model Act) · **Batch:** G (Assurance, internal control and board delegation)

**Original provisional answer.** Register context: The fund lifecycle MUST record Drafted, Accepted, Created and InvestmentActive as distinct states. Acceptance MUST be evaluated by a versioned deterministic sponsor rule: standard fund type in the account-type register (UNB-RCN-ENT-011); all participant checks required by RR-UNB-PART-007-r1 passed; sanctions clear; current agreement version accepted; no policy-defined risk signal. Where the sponso \| proposed_answer: Build deterministic acceptance with human fallback; activate only after lawful delegation.

**Relevant requirements.** RR-ADM-003-r1, RR-ADM-002-r1, UNB-RCN-ENT-011, RR-UNB-PART-007-r1, requirements/discovery/2026-11-24-simplification-economics/data/refinements.jsonl

**Authoritative findings.**

- *established* — Corporate powers exercised by or under the direction of the board; board may delegate management of activities to any person, management company or committee if activities remain under the board's ultimate direction. [EV-G-017, §5210]
- *established* — Board committees may exercise board authority except eight listed matters; grant approval and fund acceptance are not listed. [EV-G-018, §5212(a)(1)-(8)]
- *established* — Board committees need 3+ directors; committees of the corporation cannot bind the board. [EV-G-021, §712]
- *established* — Corporation managed by its board except as certificate provides. [EV-G-020, §701(a)]
- *established* — Business and affairs managed by or under the direction of the board; committees per resolution/bylaws; applies to nonstock corporations. [EV-G-016, §141(a),(c)(2),(j)]
- *established* — Powers exercised by or under the authority of the board; officers' duties per bylaws or board resolution; committees may not authorize distributions or fundamental transactions. [EV-G-023, §§8.01(b), 8.25(e), 8.41]

**Supporting interpretation.** Nonprofit statutes in the sampled states (CA, NY, DE, Model-Act state VT) let boards act 'by or under the direction of' the board and delegate to officers, committees or agents while retaining oversight; fund acceptance and grant approval are not on any non-delegable list found. A deterministic policy is not a legal person: the lawful structure is a board resolution adopting the policy and delegating its execution to a named officer or committee who remains accountable, with board oversight reporting. 'Committee' authority limits (director-only membership, 2+ in CA, 3+ in NY) apply only when a board committee is used. California's 'distributions' bar for committees (VT §8.25(e)) concerns distributions to members, not charitable grants (interpretation). Jurisdiction table: per sponsor state of incorporation record (1) board-direction statute, (2) delegation clause, (3) committee composition minimum and non-delegable list, (4) officer statute, (5) articles/bylaws restrictions. Conservative default: delegate to a named officer by board resolution, report monthly to the board, never to a non-director 'committee of the corporation'.

**Competing interpretation.** 

**Applicability to Unbound.** Fund acceptance rule tier.

**Business assumptions.**

- none recorded

**Recommended answer.** Yes in the sampled states, subject to the sponsor's articles and bylaws: the board may adopt the acceptance policy and delegate its execution to a named officer or board committee under its ultimate direction. Build deterministic acceptance with human fallback; activate the A3 outcome only after the board resolution is recorded.

**Remaining uncertainty.** States outside the sample; sponsor bylaws; RMNCA 3rd-edition text not fetched.

**Consequences if wrong.** Acceptances voidable/ultra vires; governance finding.

**Conservative fallback.** AcceptRecommended plus one-click officer batch approval.

**Alternative business or architecture design.** Officer batch approval (cost: minutes/day) vs full automation (requires resolution).

**External authorization required.** Sponsor board resolution adopting the acceptance policy version and delegating execution to a named officer/committee; bylaws check by sponsor.

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given no recorded board resolution, when the rule passes, then outcome is AcceptRecommended, not Accepted.
- Given a recorded resolution naming an officer and policy version, when the rule passes, then Accepted is attributed to that officer's delegated authority.

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- Sponsor incorporated in a state not in the table.

**Related questions.**

- none

**Corrections to prior records.**

- none

### RR-OPS-004-r1#Q1 — Auditor/CPA acceptance (SPD-PLT-001)

**Disposition:** external-authorization-required · **Confidence:** medium · **Jurisdiction:** US · **Batch:** G (Assurance, internal control and board delegation)

**Original provisional answer.** Register context: Each active control MUST have an owner role and a backup role, each resolving to at least one named person at all times. Controls classified Key (money movement, authority and delegation, financial close and reconciliation, access management) and all Manual controls MUST also have an independent reviewer role whose holders are disjoint from the owner role's holders for that control, enforced by th \| proposed_answer: Continuous evidence; replace manual procedures only after auditor acceptance.

**Relevant requirements.** RR-OPS-004-r1, SPD-PLT-001, requirements/discovery/2026-11-24-simplification-economics/data/refinements.jsonl, requirements/discovery/2026-11-24-initial-38-question-resolutions.md

**Authoritative findings.**

- *established* — Where segregation is not practical, management designs alternative control activities. [EV-G-015, paras 10.21, 10.23 (p.73)]
- *established* — Separate responsibilities so that one individual does not control all critical stages of an IT process, e.g. designing, testing and implementing. [EV-G-015, para 11.16 (p.78)]
- *established* — If GITCs are not properly designed or implemented, the auditor may not rely on automated controls in affected applications. [EV-G-010, GITC section]
- *interpretation* — Automated controls may be relied on with effective change controls without repeated testing (application material). [EV-G-012, unverified pinpoint]

**Supporting interpretation.** Independent reviewer roles for key manual controls align with monitoring and segregation norms (COSO principle 10/12, Green Book 10.21-10.23). Duplicate of SPD-PLT-001 on the auditor-acceptance gate; see master dossier.

**Competing interpretation.** 

**Applicability to Unbound.** Applies to the control register and operating model for R1.

**Business assumptions.**

- none recorded

**Recommended answer.** Continuous control evidence is acceptable in principle; no auditing or COSO source located requires an independent reviewer for every control. Keep independent review for Key and Manual controls as designed; replace manual review procedures only after the sponsor's auditor confirms which it will rely on.

**Remaining uncertainty.** Auditor's acceptance of specific evidence forms; AU-C 330 application text not fetched.

**Consequences if wrong.** Auditor requires manual sign-offs retroactively; evidence gaps for prior periods cannot be recreated.

**Conservative fallback.** Keep owner attestation for Key controls in the first audited year, then remove after auditor confirmation.

**Alternative business or architecture design.** Produce both machine evidence and a lightweight quarterly owner acknowledgment for Key controls in year 1 (cost: ~minutes per control per quarter; friction low; removes retroactive-evidence risk).

**External authorization required.** Sponsor external auditor confirmation of the evidence it will rely on.

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given a Key control, when the owner and reviewer role holders overlap, then assignment is rejected.
- Given a machine-evaluated control with a Fail result, when the due period lapses without action, then an escalation work item is opened.
- Given a policy change classified material, when the author attempts approval, then it is refused.

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- Auditor management letter comment on control evidence.

**Related questions.**

- **SPD-PLT-001**: See master dossier: evidence from inception; SOC scope by actual reliance.

**Corrections to prior records.**

- none

### RR-REQ-X-032-r1#Q1 — Does any state or program term require a periodic statement delivery to the donor?

**Disposition:** established-by-authoritative-evidence · **Confidence:** medium · **Jurisdiction:** US · **Batch:** G (Assurance, internal control and board delegation)

**Original provisional answer.** Register context: Unbound MUST generate a statement for every eligible fund for each sponsor-configured statement period (default monthly) as a pure query over the certified close snapshot (REQ-X-040), and MUST publish it to the Document Center (REQ-X-035) without human review unless the close recorded an exception affecting that fund. Notification of availability MUST follow the recipient's communication preferenc \| proposed_answer: Configurable delivery obligations, paper and accessible alternatives.

**Relevant requirements.** RR-REQ-X-032-r1, REQ-X-032, REQ-X-035, REQ-X-040, SPD-ENT-003, SPD-CFG-002, RCD-020, requirements/discovery/2026-11-24-simplification-economics/data/refinements.jsonl

**Authoritative findings.**

- *established* — DAF deduction requires a contemporaneous written acknowledgment of the sponsor's exclusive legal control; no periodic statement requirement. [EV-G-030, §170(f)(18)(B)]
- *established* — $250+ gifts require contemporaneous written acknowledgment. [EV-G-030, §170(f)(8)]
- *established* — E-SIGN consent applies where a law requires information be provided in writing to a consumer. [EV-G-031, §7001(c)(1)]
- *established* — Vanguard Charitable issues quarterly statements, January statement summarises prior year, electronic default; errors to be reported within 60 days. [EV-G-033, Statements section]
- *established* — NPT posts a quarterly statement to GivingPoint. [EV-G-034, p.17]
- *established* — Fidelity Charitable sends statements to the Primary Account Holder; no frequency stated. [EV-G-032, p.4]

**Supporting interpretation.** No federal statute or regulation found requiring periodic DAF account statements to donors; obligations arise from the sponsor's own program terms (contract). No state law requiring them was found, but a 50-state search was not done. Method: per state check charitable-solicitation and consumer-protection statutes for donor-reporting duties; conservative default: whatever the program terms promise is mandatory. E-SIGN consent is needed only for legally required writings (e.g. contribution acknowledgments), not for contractual statements.

**Competing interpretation.** 

**Applicability to Unbound.** Statement generation and notification.

**Business assumptions.**

- none recorded

**Recommended answer.** No federal law requires periodic DAF statements; none found at state level (sample, not exhaustive). Program terms create the duty (Vanguard and NPT promise quarterly). Make delivery obligations configurable per sponsor program terms, with paper and accessible alternatives.

**Remaining uncertainty.** State-level donor-reporting rules not exhaustively searched; Schwab terms not retrievable.

**Consequences if wrong.** Breach of program terms; donor complaints.

**Conservative fallback.** Quarterly notification with statement for all funds.

**Alternative business or architecture design.** Publish-only with in-app availability (lowest cost) vs mailed paper (highest cost).

**External authorization required.** none

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given program terms promising quarterly statements, when the quarter closes, then each eligible donor receives notice within the configured days.
- Given no e-consent on file for a legally required acknowledgment, then paper is sent.

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- State enacts DAF donor-reporting law; final §4966 regs add reporting.

**Related questions.**

- **SPD-ENT-003**: No legal requirement found; program terms govern.

**Corrections to prior records.**

- RR-REQ-X-032-r1 default monthly: Monthly default exceeds observed large-sponsor practice (quarterly). → Distinguish generation (monthly OK) from notification (quarterly default).

### RR-REQ-X-073-r1#Q1 — Are the 3/12-month thresholds acceptable to the sponsor's auditor?

**Disposition:** reasoned-interpretation · **Confidence:** medium · **Jurisdiction:** US · **Batch:** G (Assurance, internal control and board delegation)

**Original provisional answer.** Register context: Unbound MUST maintain a critical-vendor register recording assurance type, legal entity and report period; MUST require a bridge letter when the period end is more than 3 months old and mark assurance Expired after 12 months; and MUST map every complementary user entity control to an internal control owner before the vendor is marked Assured. AI may extract report metadata for human confirmation o \| proposed_answer: Treat thresholds as provisional; configurable only within approved control policy. \| hold_resolution: Source: requirements/discovery/2026-11-23-cross-industry/data/refinements.jsonl, parent REQ-X-073. Approve 3-month bridge request and 12-month stale-report alert as provisional monitoring defaults, not universal audit standards. Bridge letters do not extend independent assurance. Track coverage gap, report scope, opinion, exceptions, complementary controls and time-limited risk acceptance independently. Critical vendor suspension requires risk-based continuity decision. Test bridge letter with unmapped CUEC, adverse opinion, expired risk acceptance and vendor outage. Auditor confirms reliance criteria.

**Relevant requirements.** RR-REQ-X-073-r1, REQ-X-073, requirements/discovery/2026-11-23-cross-industry/data/refinements.jsonl:14, research/daf-theoretical-enterprise-validation/19-questions/VENDOR-ASSURANCE-SOC-CONTROL-OWNERSHIP-2026-11-23.md, requirements/discovery/2026-11-24-three-evidence-hold-resolutions.md

**Authoritative findings.**

- *established* — A bridge letter is signed by service-organization management, not the service auditor, and provides no attestation over the gap period. [EV-G-008, definition and signer sections]
- *established* — AICPA guidance does not set bridge-letter requirements. [EV-G-008, AICPA note]
- *established* — Most bridge letters cover no more than three months; SOC reports typically cover 6-12 months and recur at least annually. [EV-G-008, duration section]
- *established* — No formal guidance; auditors generally would not accept a bridge letter covering more than three months. [EV-G-009, duration passage]
- *established* — User auditor evaluates the period covered and time elapsed, and may obtain additional evidence for the remaining period. [EV-G-001, para 17(c); A34]

**Supporting interpretation.** Bridge letters are management assertions, not assurance. Three months is a practice tolerance; 12 months is consistent with annual report cycles but is not a standard. The relevant gap is between the vendor report period end and the user entity's (sponsor's) fiscal year-end or the reliance date, not report age alone.

**Competing interpretation.** 

**Applicability to Unbound.** Unbound's own critical-vendor register (and sponsors relying on Unbound's reports).

**Business assumptions.**

- none recorded

**Recommended answer.** Keep 3-month bridge request and 12-month stale-report alert as configurable monitoring defaults, labelled practice not standard. Compute the gap against each relying party's fiscal year-end/reliance date. Never treat a bridge letter as extending assurance; require compensating procedures or risk acceptance beyond 3 months. Auditor confirms reliance criteria.

**Remaining uncertainty.** Practice sources only (levels 10); AICPA silent.

**Consequences if wrong.** Over- or under-triggering vendor work; false comfort.

**Conservative fallback.** Shorter gap tolerance for critical vendors and direct inquiry with evidence.

**Alternative business or architecture design.** Request vendors align report period ends to the sponsor fiscal year-end (no bridge needed; cost: negotiation only).

**External authorization required.** Sponsor external auditor confirms tolerance used in its reliance.

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given a SOC 2 period ending 5 months before the sponsor fiscal year-end and no bridge letter, when evaluated, then status BridgeRequired.
- Given a bridge letter, when assurance status is computed, then the gap is shown as management assertion, not Assured.
- Given a report period end older than 12 months, then status Expired and a risk-acceptance work item.

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- AICPA issues guidance on bridge letters.
- Auditor states different tolerance.

**Related questions.**

- none

**Corrections to prior records.**

- RR-REQ-X-073-r1 statement: Triggers bridge letter by 'period end more than 3 months old' irrespective of the relying party's year-end. → Measure gap from report period end to relying party's fiscal year-end or reliance date; label thresholds as practice defaults.

### RR-SOC2-024-r1#Q1 — Auditor/CPA acceptance (SPD-PLT-001)

**Disposition:** external-authorization-required · **Confidence:** medium · **Jurisdiction:** US · **Batch:** G (Assurance, internal control and board delegation)

**Original provisional answer.** Register context: Each control in the register MUST be classified MachineEvaluated or Manual. A MachineEvaluated control MUST be evaluated continuously by MON-005 over its complete expected population (MON-002), and its owner MUST act on every Fail, InsufficientEvidence or StaleEvidence result within the control's due period; no periodic owner attestation is required for its passing results. A Manual control (e.g. \| proposed_answer: Evidence engineering from inception; assurance timing driven by contracts and auditor reliance.

**Relevant requirements.** RR-SOC2-024-r1, SPD-PLT-001, requirements/discovery/2026-11-24-simplification-economics/data/refinements.jsonl, requirements/discovery/2026-11-24-initial-38-question-resolutions.md

**Authoritative findings.**

- *established* — Where segregation is not practical, management designs alternative control activities. [EV-G-015, paras 10.21, 10.23 (p.73)]
- *established* — Separate responsibilities so that one individual does not control all critical stages of an IT process, e.g. designing, testing and implementing. [EV-G-015, para 11.16 (p.78)]
- *established* — If GITCs are not properly designed or implemented, the auditor may not rely on automated controls in affected applications. [EV-G-010, GITC section]
- *interpretation* — Automated controls may be relied on with effective change controls without repeated testing (application material). [EV-G-012, unverified pinpoint]

**Supporting interpretation.** Automated controls tend to be more reliable (Green Book 10.05); reliance depends on effective GITCs (SAS 145). Duplicate of SPD-PLT-001 on the auditor-acceptance gate; see master dossier.

**Competing interpretation.** 

**Applicability to Unbound.** Applies to the control register and operating model for R1.

**Business assumptions.**

- none recorded

**Recommended answer.** Machine-evaluated controls without periodic owner attestation are consistent with how auditors rely on automated controls (effective ITGCs plus single-instance or baseline testing). Retain owner action on every Fail/InsufficientEvidence. Auditor confirms scope.

**Remaining uncertainty.** Auditor's acceptance of specific evidence forms; AU-C 330 application text not fetched.

**Consequences if wrong.** Auditor requires manual sign-offs retroactively; evidence gaps for prior periods cannot be recreated.

**Conservative fallback.** Keep owner attestation for Key controls in the first audited year, then remove after auditor confirmation.

**Alternative business or architecture design.** Produce both machine evidence and a lightweight quarterly owner acknowledgment for Key controls in year 1 (cost: ~minutes per control per quarter; friction low; removes retroactive-evidence risk).

**External authorization required.** Sponsor external auditor confirmation of the evidence it will rely on.

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given a Key control, when the owner and reviewer role holders overlap, then assignment is rejected.
- Given a machine-evaluated control with a Fail result, when the due period lapses without action, then an escalation work item is opened.
- Given a policy change classified material, when the author attempts approval, then it is refused.

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- Auditor management letter comment on control evidence.

**Related questions.**

- **SPD-PLT-001**: See master dossier: evidence from inception; SOC scope by actual reliance.

**Corrections to prior records.**

### RR-UNB-ACC-017-r1#Q1 — External auditor accepts generated evidence in lieu of checklist.

**Disposition:** reasoned-interpretation · **Confidence:** medium · **Jurisdiction:** US · **Batch:** G (Assurance, internal control and board delegation)

**Original provisional answer.** Register context: Each legal entity MUST have accounting periods with soft and hard close, period locks (UNB-XIN-EVA-002) and late-event routing (XID-EVA-002) without modifying posted journals. Close certification MUST be granted when continuous invariants (UNB-ACC-030) pass, reconciliation breaks above materiality are resolved (RR-UNB-ACC-018-r1) and the period sample is attested (RCD-037); the system MUST generat \| proposed_answer: Replace manual checklists only when auditor accepts equivalent generated evidence.

**Relevant requirements.** RR-UNB-ACC-017-r1, UNB-ACC-030, RCD-037, requirements/discovery/2026-11-24-simplification-economics/data/refinements.jsonl, requirements/discovery/2026-11-24-simplification-economics/18-REFINED-REQUIREMENTS.md

**Authoritative findings.**

- *established* — SAS 145 requires identifying IT applications, risks arising from IT and the GITCs addressing them; ineffective GITCs may preclude reliance on automated controls. [EV-G-010, GITC section]
- *established* — Reliance on system-generated reports may require testing their completeness and accuracy and related GITCs. [EV-G-010, report reliance passage]
- *established* — SAS 142 (AU-C 500) requires evaluating reliability of information regardless of source: accuracy, completeness, authenticity, management bias. [EV-G-011, reliability passage]
- *established* — Issuer benchmarking (AS 2201 B28-B33): an automated control may be concluded effective without retesting if ITGCs are effective and configuration unchanged. [EV-G-013, benchmarking section]
- *interpretation* — Automated control evidence plus effective change controls may support operating effectiveness (AU-C/ISA 330 application material). [EV-G-012, unverified]

**Supporting interpretation.** Auditors can accept generated close evidence in place of manual checklists, provided (a) ITGCs over the platform (access, change management, operations) are effective and evidenced, and (b) the generated reports are shown complete and accurate (IPE). A checklist is not itself required by any standard; it is evidence of performance, which generated certification can provide.

**Competing interpretation.** 

**Applicability to Unbound.** Close certification, invariants, reconciliation-break evidence.

**Business assumptions.**

- none recorded

**Recommended answer.** Approve: replace manual close checklists with generated close certification when the auditor accepts it; build IPE support (query definition, parameters, population counts, hash, run log) into every generated report; keep change-management evidence for invariant code. Auditor confirms in year 1.

**Remaining uncertainty.** AU-C 330/500 primary text not fetched; individual auditor practice varies.

**Consequences if wrong.** Auditor re-performs close procedures; higher fees.

**Conservative fallback.** Run generated evidence alongside a one-page signed close attestation in year 1.

**Alternative business or architecture design.** Export full populations for auditor data analytics (SAS 142 recognises full-population techniques) rather than samples.

**External authorization required.** Sponsor external auditor acceptance of generated evidence.

**Acceptance criteria.**

- Given a generated close report, when exported, then it includes query version, parameters, row counts, hash and run timestamp.
- Given a code change to an invariant, when deployed, then change ticket, approver distinct from author, and test evidence exist.

**Reconsideration triggers.**

- Auditor rejects generated evidence.

**Related questions.**

- **RR-UNB-GOV-002-r1#Q1**: Independent approval of material policy changes supports ITGC change-management reliance.

**Corrections to prior records.**

- none
